Berndtsson-Lempert-Szőke Fields associated to Proper Holomorphic Families of Vector Bundles Thanks: Mathematics Subject Classification (2020 Database): 32L05
Dror Varolin

Abstract
. Drawing on work of Berndtsson and of Lempert and Szőke, we define a complex analytic structure for families of (possibly finite-dimensional) Hilbert spaces that might not fit together to form a holomorphic vector bundle but nevertheless have a reasonable definition of curvature that agrees with the curvature of the Chern connection when the family of Hilbert spaces is locally trivial. As one consequence, we obtain a new proof of a theorem of Berndtsson on the curvature of direct images of semi-positively twisted relative canonical bundles (i.e., adjoint bundles), and of its higher-rank generalization by Liu-Yang.



Dedicated to Bo Berndtsson and Laszlo Lempert

In the present article a
holomorphic family
of complex manifolds is a surjective holomorphic submersion
p:X\to B
. The holomorphic family is said to be
proper
if
p:X\to B
is proper. The holomorphic family is said to be
Kähler
if it admits a
relative Kähler form
, i.e., a closed real
(1,1)
-form
\omega
on the total space
X
, whose pullback to every fiber is Kähler.

1.
Introduction

Let
p:X\to B
be a holomorphic family, and denote by
m
and
n
the dimension of
B
and the fiber dimension of
p
respectively. Let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with smooth Hermitian metric
h
. We study the family of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
whose fiber over
t\in B
is

{\mathscr{H}}_{t}:=\left\{f\in H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{t}}(K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))\right\},

where
X_{t}:=p^{-1}(t)
, and whose Hermitian metric on the fiber
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
is

(1)

(f_{1},f_{2})_{t}:=\int_{X_{t}}\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\left<f_{1}\wedge\bar{f}_{2},h\right>.

The study of such families of Hilbert spaces was initiated in
[
B-2009
]
by Berndtsson, who considered the case in which
E
is a line bundle. Berndtsson gives such families of Hilbert spaces a complex geometric structure by defining notions of smooth sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
and a
\bar{\partial}
operator for such sections. He notes that in general such structures do not yield vector bundles, even when the family
p:X\to B
is proper. (Some natural examples are discussed in Paragraph
5.3
.) However, even when not locally trivial,
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
resembles a holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle in certain ways.

In the present article, we mostly study proper holomorphic families of complex manifolds. We propose a definition of the curvature endomorphism of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
(Definition
4.19
). Our definition agrees with the usual definition if
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle, i.e., it is locally trivial. Our first result is the following theorem.

Theorem 1
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family of Hermitian holomorphic vector bundles and let
k\geq 1
be an integer. If the metric
h
is
k
-non-negatively curved (resp.
k
-positively curved) then
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is
k
-non-negatively curved (resp.
k
-positively curved).

The notion of
k
-positivity was introduced by Demailly in
[
D-1982
, Définition 2.2]
. Metrics are
1
-positively curved if and only if they are positively curved in the sense of Griffiths, while metrics are
k
-positively curved for every
k
if and only if they are positively curved in the sense of Nakano. If
E
is a line bundle then all of these notions are equivalent. Notions of positivity are recalled in Paragraph
2.12
.

By taking
k\geq\min(n,r)
where
r={\rm Rank}(E)
and
n
is the fiber dimension of
p
, one recovers a result first proved by Mourougane and Takayama
[
MT-2008
, Theorem 1.1,
q=0
case]
.

Except in the case
k\geq\min(n,r)
, we do not know if the hypotheses of Theorem
1
imply that
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a holomorphic vector bundle. At present the main tool for local triviality is the
L^{2}
Extension package of Ohsawa-Takegoshi type. (See Paragraph
5.1
.) To apply the
L^{2}
Extension Theorem, the vector bundle
E\to X
must admit a metric with non-negative curvature in the sense of Nakano. The hypotheses in the next two corollaries yield such metrics.

Corollary 1.1

(
[
B-2009
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
(E,e^{-\varphi})\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family of holomorphic Hermitian line bundles. If the curvature of
(E,e^{-\varphi})\to X
is non-negative (resp. positive) then
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a holomorphic vector bundle and the curvature of the Chern connection of the
L^{2}
metric (
1
) is non-negative (resp. positive) in the sense of Nakano.

The next corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem
1
, and local triviality holds because Nakano positivity of the metric
h
on fibers implies that for a sufficiently plurisubharmonic function
\psi
on an open subset
U\subset B
the metric
e^{-p^{*}\psi}h
for
E|_{U}
is Nakano positive.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles and let
k\geq 1
be an integer. Assume that for all
t\in B
the metrics
h_{t}=h|_{X_{t}}
are Nakano-positive, and that the metric
h
is
k
-non-negatively (resp.
k
-positively) curved. Then the holomorphic vector bundle
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is
k
-non-negatively (resp.
k
-positively) curved.

Remark 1.3
.

One can weaken the hypotheses of Corollary
1.2
: instead of assuming that the metric
h
is fiberwise-Nakano positive, one can assume that

\Theta(h)\geq-Cp^{*}\omega\otimes{\rm Id}_{E}

for some positive
(1,1)
-form
\omega
on
B
and some continuous
C:X\to[0,\infty)
. If the fibers are projective (or more generally essentially Stein) then one can even just assume that
h
is fiberwise-nonnegative, though in that case the proof requires a bit more work. These hypotheses also imply that
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is locally trivial, and then the result follows from Theorem
1
. However, we have chosen to state Corollary
1.2
with the stronger hypotheses because in Section
9
we give a different, more direct proof. Our proof uses Hörmander’s
L^{2}
estimate and twisted Lefschetz theory (both of which are recalled in Section
6
), as well as Theorem
2
, which we formulate next.

\diamond

Theorem
1
can be strengthened. Indeed, there is always a non-negative contribution to the curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
from the deformation-theoretic properties of the proper holomorphic family
p:X\to B
, and this contribution can be positive when the family is not locally trivial. That this is so was already observed by Berndtsson in
[
B-2009
]
and then quantified in
[
B-2011
]
and later by Liu and Yang in
[
LY-2014
]
, who were the first to consider the case of higher rank
E
. In all cases the extra positivity is a consequence of formulas for the curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
. (Liu and Yang also obtained a number of other equivalent curvature formulas and discussed several interesting applications of these formulas.)

In
[
B-2011
]
and
[
LY-2014
]
the curvature formula is obtained under the assumption of local triviality of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
, which makes the computation more manageable. In the non-locally trivial case the derivation of the curvature formula is necessarily more complicated. To make its meaning maximally clear, we obtain the curvature formula in a pair of theorems, whose statements make use of the following notation.

i.

A
horizontal distribution
for
p:X\to B
is a (not necessarily integrable) subbundle
\theta\subset T_{X}
such that
dp|_{\theta}:\theta\to T_{B}
is a vector bundle isomorphism. Such distributions
\theta
are in one-to-one correspondence with
horizontal lifts
, i.e., linear maps that assign to a
(1,0)
-vector
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
a
(1,0)
-vector field
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\in\Gamma(X_{t},T^{1,0}_{X}|_{X_{t}})
such that
dp\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}=\tau
. (See Paragraph
3.3
.)

ii.

Let
t\in B
be fixed. For
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
we define

\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}:{\mathscr{H}}_{t}\to H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n-1,1}_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\tau}:{\mathscr{H}}_{t}\to H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))

by

\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f:=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\tau}f:=P_{t}^{\perp}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}u),

where
u\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
is any section such that
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=f
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u\equiv 0
. Here
P_{t}^{\perp}\!:H^{0}(X_{t},L^{2}_{\ell oc}(K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))\to{\mathscr{H}}_{t}^{\perp}
is the orthogonal projection and

L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\alpha:=\nabla^{1,0}(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\alpha)+\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\alpha,

is the
twisted Lie derivative
of an
E
-valued differential form
\alpha
associated to the Chern connection
\nabla=\nabla^{1,0}+\bar{\partial}
for
(E,h)\to X
. (See paragraph
3.2
.) As we will see later, these definitions are independent of the choice of
u
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles. Then the curvature
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is given by

(2)

(\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2})=\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2}\right)-\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>-\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>,

where
\theta
is any horizontal distribution,
t\in B
,
\sigma,\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
,
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
,
\Theta(h)
is the curvature of
(E,h)\to X
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the inner product (
1
).

We emphasize that the curvature
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
is independent of the choice of
\theta
.

Remark 1.4
.

Each of the three terms on the right hand side of Formula (
2
), while dependent on the choice of horizontal distribution
\theta
, is independent of the way in which the
(1,0)
-vectors
\sigma
and
\tau
are extended to
(1,0)
-vector fields. We therefore adopt the inessential but simplifying convention that these vectors are extended locally holomorphically, i.e.,
\sigma,\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}(T^{1,0}_{B})_{t}
.

\diamond

The precise definition of the curvature
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
requires some technology that we collect or develop in Sections
2
,
3
and
4
. But with Theorem
2
now stated, we can explain the basic idea behind the definition of
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
. Consider the family of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
whose fiber over
t\in B
is

{\mathscr{L}}_{t}:=\left\{f\in H^{0}(X_{t},L^{2}_{\ell oc}(K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))\right\},

equipped with the same inner product (
1
) used to define
{\mathscr{H}}
. The curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}
can be defined because
{\mathscr{L}}
is reasonably well-behaved: it is a smooth Hilbert field in the sense of Lempert-Szőke.

Evidently the fibers of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
are subspaces of the fibers of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
. Each horizontal distribution
\theta
defines an operator
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
on smooth sections of
{\mathscr{L}}
(Definition
4.12
). Remarkably, for every
\theta
the operator
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
, when restricted to sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
, agrees with the
\bar{\partial}
-operator of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
introduced by Berndtsson (Definition
4.6
). Therefore
({\mathscr{H}},\bar{\partial})
is a ‘holomorphic sub-field’ of
({\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})
.

If
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is locally trivial then a Gauss-Griffiths-type formula links the curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}
, the curvature of
({\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})
restricted to
{\mathscr{H}}
, and the second fundamental form of
{\mathscr{H}}\subset({\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})
(Proposition
2.32
).

One can also define the second fundamental form in
{\mathscr{H}}
even when
{\mathscr{H}}
is not locally trivial. This definition yields the curvature as an endomorphism of
{\mathscr{H}}
viewed as a
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B}
-module. Unlike the finite-rank case, such an endomorphism is not automatically represented by a map of Hilbert fields. To have such a map one must be able to extend a dense subset of every fiber
{\mathscr{H}}
to sections of
{\mathscr{L}}
with particular special properties. The details are described abstractly in Paragraph
2.11
, and then more concretely in Theorem
4.17
for
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}
.

From the Gauss Formula perspective the sum of the first two terms on the right hand side of the identity (
2
) is precisely the restriction to
{\mathscr{H}}
of the curvature of
({\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})
(Theorem
8.5
), while the last term on the right hand side of (
2
) is the negative of the ‘square of the second fundamental form’.

Remark 1.5
.

By Ehresmann’s Theorem the properness of the holomorphic family
p:X\to B
implies that the field of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
has a natural structure of a smooth Hilbert bundle, i.e., it is locally trivial.

Here we are more concerned with the complex structure, which is required for any notion of Chern connection. From this perspective, we have not addressed here the interesting question of whether for
some
horizontal distribution
\theta
the field of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
has the structure of an almost holomorphic vector bundle whose
\bar{\partial}
-operator is
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
. We suspect that this is rarely the case.

Instead we circumvent this question by introducing the notion of Berndtsson-Lempert-Szőke (BLS) fields. This notion combines Berndtsson’s complex structures with the notion of smooth structure introduced by Lempert and Szőke. The notion is general beyond the case of proper holomorphic families. One can give meaning to curvature in the setting of families of complex manifolds-with-boundary, as has recently been done by Upadrashta
[
U-2021
]
, whose work significantly generalizes some of the elegant results of X. Wang
[
Wang-2017
]
.

\diamond

As stated in Remark
1.3
, Theorem
2
yields a proof of Corollary
1.2
that does not pass through Theorem
1
. To complete such a proof one must estimate the second and third terms on the right hand side of the curvature formula (
2
). The third term can be estimated using basic inequalities and a direct computation that is independent of the horizontal distribution
\theta
. To handle the second term, one wants to have the equality

(3)

-\int_{X_{t}}(\text{-}1)^{\tfrac{n^{2}}{2}}\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>=\left(\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1},\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}\right),

where the right hand side is the inner product induced on
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-forms on
X_{t}
by the metric
h
and any Kähler metric on the fiber
X_{t}
. The equality (
3
) does not hold for all horizontal lifts
\theta
, but such a
\theta
exists on any Kähler family.

Remark
.

A previous draft of the article had a more complicated approach to finding
\theta
. We are grateful to the anonymous referee for pointing out that horizontal lift
\theta
defined by
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\omega=0
, where
\omega
is the relative Kähler form, has the required property.

\diamond

The identity (
3
), while handy for a proof of Corollary
1.2
(see Paragraph
9.2
), is not required in our proof of Theorem
1
. Instead one uses the Hodge Theorem in a different way to compute an exact formula for the square of the second fundamental form, i.e., the third term on the right hand side of (
2
). The statement is as follows.

Theorem 3
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a Kähler family of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles, let
\theta
be a smooth horizontal distribution, let
t\in B
and let
\omega_{t}
be a Kähler form on
X_{t}
. Then for every
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
there exist smooth section
u_{1},u_{2}\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
, depending on
\theta
and
\omega_{t}
and satisfying
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u_{i}=f_{i}
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u_{i}=0
,
i=1,2
, such that

\displaystyle-\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>=\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>+\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{2}\right)

(4)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h)\right)f_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u_{2}\right)+\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1},\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h)\right)f_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\left(\left[\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\right](\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1}),(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u_{2})\right)

for all
\sigma,\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
. The pairings
(\cdot,\cdot)
in the last four terms on the right hand side of (
3
) are the
L^{2}
inner products induced on
E|_{X_{t}}
-valued forms by the Hermitian metric
h_{t}
and the Kähler metric
\omega_{t}
.

The aforementioned use of the Hodge Theorem in the proof of Theorem
3
is equivalent to a construction of Berndtsson, formalized below as Theorem
9.2
. With Theorem
9.2
in hand, the rest of the proof of Theorem
3
is a somewhat involved integration-by-parts argument.

Finally, Theorem
1
is follows from Theorems
2
and
3
and the formula

\displaystyle\left<p_{*}\left(\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\left<(\Theta(h)u_{1}\wedge\overline{u_{2}},h\right>\right),\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>

\displaystyle\quad=\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2}\right)+\left(\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h)\right)f_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u_{2}\right)+\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1},\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h)\right)f_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\left(\left[\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega_{t}}\right](\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1}),(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u_{2})\right),

which we establish in Section
9
(Proposition
9.3
). Indeed, together with Theorems
2
and
3
, the latter identity yields

(5)

(\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2})=\left<p_{*}\left(\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\left<(\Theta(h)\wedge u_{1})\wedge\overline{u_{2}},h\right>\right),\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>+\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{2}\right).

If
{\mathscr{H}}
is locally trivial Formula (
5
) is due to Berndtsson
[
B-2011
, Section 2]
if
{\rm Rank}(E)=1
and to Liu-Yang
[
LY-2014
, Theorem 1.6]
in the higher rank case. The term

\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{2}\right)

on the right hand side of (
5
) gives the extra positivity for the improvement of Theorem
1
mentioned after the statement of Corollary
1.2
. This term and the term
\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>
are related to deformation theory of compact complex manifolds, and more specifically to the Kodaira-Spencer map, which captures the first obstruction to local triviality of holomorphic families. (See Section
7
for more detail.)

We end this introduction with an explanation of one of our initial motivations. If the family
X\to B
is trivial then Corollary
1.1
has a considerably simpler proof. In fact, this same simplified proof yields a significantly more general result, stated as
[
B-2009
, Theorem 1.1]
. By our reading of
[
B-2009
]
, there is a problem implicitly stated in the second-to-last paragraph of the introduction to
[
B-2009
]
: whether one can prove
[
B-2009
, Theorem 1.2]
(i.e. Corollary
1.1
above) in a manner similar to the proof of
[
B-2009
, Theorem 1.1]
. Berndtsson states there that he was not “… able to find a natural complex structure on the space of all (not necessarily holomorphic)
(n,0)
forms, extending the complex structure on [
{\mathscr{H}}
].” In fact, there are infinitely many consistent ways to extend the complex structure of
{\mathscr{H}}
to
{\mathscr{L}}
, each one corresponding to a choice of horizontal lift
\theta
. The catch is that the resulting object is not a holomorphic vector bundle, and a central point of the present article is to deal with the lack of local triviality.

Aside from a solution of Berndtsson’s implicit problem, we obtain a somewhat different geometric perspective. In BLS geometry the Kodaira-Spencer class affects both the curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}
and the second fundamental form of
{\mathscr{H}}
in
{\mathscr{L}}
. In the curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}
one sees the Hodge-Riemann ‘square’

-\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>

of a certain representative
\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f
of the action on
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms of of the Kodaira-Spencer map of
p:X\to B
; a representative that depends only on the choice of complex structure
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
for
{\mathscr{L}}
, while in the second fundamental form of
{\mathscr{H}}
in
{\mathscr{L}}
one sees the “difference of squares”

-\int_{X_{t}}\!\!\!\!\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\!\!\left<\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f_{1}\wedge\overline{\kappa^{\theta}_{\tau}f_{2}},h\right>-\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{2}\right)

of two representatives of the Kodaira-Spencer map. The first of these is the term appearing in the curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}
, and as already mentioned it depends only on the choice of complex structure for
{\mathscr{L}}
. On the other hand, the other representative

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=\kappa^{\theta}_{\sigma}f-\bar{\partial}\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner u)\right)

of the action of the Kodaira-Spencer map depends on how elements
f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
are extended to sections
u
of
{\mathscr{L}}
. Only the term
\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{2}\right)
survives in the formula (
5
) for
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
.

In the same penultimate paragraph of the introduction of
[
B-2009
]
it is mentioned that the Kodaira-Spencer class of
p:X\to B
“plays somewhat the role of another second fundamental form, but this time of a quotient bundle, …”. It is certainly intriguing to try to realize
{\mathscr{H}}
is quotient bundle instead of a subbundle. We have thus far been unable to find such a construction.

Outline of the article

To help the reader navigate our rather long article, we summarize the content of the paper as follows.

(2)

We present the abstract theory of BLS fields.

(3)

We discuss the relative canonical bundle, and then we carry out some explicit calculations that will be useful in various proofs. We also discuss horizontal distributions.

(4)

We begin by recalling Berndtsson’s construction of what we have called Berndtsson’s Hilbert field. We then introduce the
L^{2}
BLS fields, and the iBLS structure of the Berndtsson Hilbert field, and we state the result on lift-independence of the relative curvatures of the Berndtsson Hilbert field with respect to the
L^{2}
BLS fields (Theorem
4.18
). After that, we define positivity for Hilbert fields; the definition is the obvious adaptation of the notions of positivity for holomorphic vector bundles to the setting of Hilbert fields. Then we establish the Schur-complement-type result (Proposition
4.22
) needed in the proof of Corollary
1.2
. We conclude with a discussion of semi-local descriptions of representatives of of sections of twisted relative canonical bundles.

(5)

We discuss the question of local triviality of the Berndtsson Hilbert field.

(6)

We collect well-known results from Hodge Theory that are needed in the proofs of Theorem
1
and Corollary
1.2
.

(7)

We discuss the Kodaira-Spencer map and its action on elements of the Berndtsson Hilbert field.

(8)

We compute (the
(1,1)
-component of) the curvatures of the Ambient Hilbert fields, from which Theorem
2
follows. We then use the computation to finally prove the theorem on lift-independence of the relative curvatures (Theorem
4.18
).

(9)

We begin by proving Corollary
1.2
. We then prove Theorem
3
using a slight generalization of a result of Berndtsson concerning the existence of good representatives of twisted relative canonical bundles (Theorem
9.2
). We conclude with an integration-by-parts computation (Proposition
9.3
) which, when combined with Theorems
2
and
3
, yields Berndtsson-Liu-Yang’s curvature formula (
5
), from which Theorem
1
follows.

Remark
.

It is important to mention that many people have studied the direct image sheaf of the relative canonical bundle twisted by a holomorphic line bundle or vector bundle. Among these, most closely related to the present article are the results of Mourougane-Takayama
[
MT-2007
,
MT-2008
]
, Berndtsson-Păun
[
BP-2008
]
, Berndtsson-Păun-Wang
[
BPW-2022
]
, and Liu-Yang
[
LY-2014
]
.

On the other hand, it might also be worth pointing out that the present article concerns a rather different situation; when not local trivial, the family
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is not so closely related to the direct image of the twisted relative canonical bundle.

\diamond

2.
Abstract Theory of Berndtsson-Lempert-Szőke Fields

In this section we discuss the general geometric structure with which
{\mathscr{H}}
and
{\mathscr{L}}
are eventually endowed. We start with a slight reorganization and minor refinement of ideas introduced by Lempert and Szőke in
[
LS-2014
]
, and then we introduce definitions that are already suggested or implied in
[
LS-2014
]
. For example, in
[
LS-2014
]
the introduction of a
\bar{\partial}
-operator to define the holomorphic structure of a smooth (quasi)-Hilbert field is not abstracted, but introduced in the ‘direct image’ constructions of
[
LS-2014
, Part II]
for the bundle of holomorphic sections. And the discussion of subfields is not as explicit, but many of the elements are hinted at in both
[
LS-2014
]
and
[
B-2009
]
. In fact,
[
B-2009
]
considers subfields in the case of trivial families of complex manifolds.

2.1.
Quasi-Hilbert fields

Definition 2.1
.

A quasi-Hilbert field is a map
\pi:{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
whose fibers are topological vector spaces each of which is isomorphic to a Hilbert space.

\diamond

That is to say, for each
t\in B
there is a Hilbert space
H_{t}
such that the fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}:=\pi^{-1}(t)
is isomorphic, as a topological vector space, to
H_{t}
, but
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
does not have a canonical inner product. The name is inspired by the fact that a linear isomorphism of two Hilbert spaces is a quasi-isometry. We shall say that the Hilbert space
H_{t}
is quasi-isometric to
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
, even though the latter does not have a canonical inner product.

Remark 2.2
.

Even if the Hilbert space fibers of a quasi-Hilbert field are finite-dimensional, one does not want all of them to be isomorphic to a single vector space. Indeed, in our setting of a holomorphic family
E\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
in which
p
is proper, the field of Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
has constant fiber dimension if and only if it is locally trivial.

\diamond

Definition 2.3
.

The dual of a quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is the quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}^{*}\to B
such that for each
t\in B
the fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}^{*}
is the topological dual of
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
, i.e., the set of bounded linear functionals on
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
.

\diamond

By the Riesz Representation Theorem each fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}^{*}
of the dual quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}^{*}
is isometric to the dual fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
. Therefore the Hilbert fields
{\mathfrak{H}}
and
{\mathfrak{H}}^{*}
are isomorphic quasi-Hilbert fields.

2.2.
Smooth structures on quasi-Hilbert fields

We denote by
\Gamma(U,{\mathfrak{H}})
the set of sections (i.e. right inverses) of
\pi:{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
over
U\subset B
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
B
be a smooth manifold and let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field.

i.

A smooth structure for a quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is a sheaf
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to B
of
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-modules on
B
, whose elements are germs of sections of
\pi:{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
, such that for each
t\in B
the stalk
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
is mapped by the evaluation-at-
t
map to a dense subspace of the Hilbert space
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
. The associated presheaf assigns to an open set
U\subset B
a set of sections of
\pi:\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
denoted
\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
, whose members are called
smooth sections over
U
.

ii.

The smooth structure for
{\mathfrak{H}}^{*}\to B
dual to the smooth structure of
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is the sheaf
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{*})\to B
associated to the presheaf whose groups
\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{*}))
are defined as follows:
\phi\in\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{*}))
if and only if for every
f\in\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
the function
\left<\phi,f\right>\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(U)
.

\diamond

2.3.
Maps

We shall often encounter maps between (smooth) quasi-Hilbert fields.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}^{1},{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\to B
be quasi-Hilbert fields.

a.

A map
F:{\mathfrak{H}}^{1}\to{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}
is said to be a
map of quasi-Hilbert fields
if it is fiber-preserving, i.e.,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{H}}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm Id}

and linear on fibers. We write
F\in{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1},{\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
.

(As is common in the study of linear transformations of Hilbert spaces, the fiber restrictions need not be continuous or even everywhere defined.) If the fiber restrictions are defined on dense subsets of the fibers we will say that
F
is
densely-defined
.

b.

A map of quasi-Hilbert fields
F:{\mathfrak{H}}^{1}\to{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}
is called a
morphism
if the restrictions to fibers are continuous. In this case we write
F\in{\rm Hom}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1},{\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
.

c.

Suppose moreover that
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})
and
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
are smooth structures for
{\mathfrak{H}}^{1}
and
{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}
respectively. A map
F\in{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1},{\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
is said to be
smooth
if it maps
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})
to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
, i.e., if for each
t\in B
one has
F{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{2})_{t}
for every
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})_{t}
.

Thus a smooth map is densely-defined. However, a smooth map need not be a morphism, and a morphism need not be smooth.

One often also encounters maps of smooth sections.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}^{1},{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\to B
be quasi-Hilbert fields with smooth structures
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})
and
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
.

(1)

A map of sheaves
\mathbf{L}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{2})
is said to be
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear
if

\mathbf{L}(f{\mathfrak{f}})=f\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}}

for each
t\in B
, all
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})_{t}
and all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B,t}
.

(2)

A
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear
\mathbf{L}
is said to be
tensorial at
t\in B
if

{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})_{t}\ \text{ and }\ {\mathfrak{f}}(t)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad(\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0.

We say
\mathbf{L}
is
tensorial
if
\mathbf{L}
is tensorial at every point of
B
.

Note that a tensorial
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear map determines a smooth map of quasi-Hilbert fields in the sense of Definition
2.5
.a. (The converse is obvious.) Indeed, if
\mathbf{L}
is a tensorial
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear map then one can define the map of quasi-Hilbert fields
L
by

L{\mathfrak{f}}(t):=(\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}})(t),\quad{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t},

and the tensorial condition means
L
is well-defined.

Remark 2.7
.

If
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}^{1})_{t}
is generated, as a
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B,t}
-module, by germs that do not vanish at
p
then every
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear map is tensorial at
t
. This is the situation for smooth finite-rank vector bundles with their usual smooth structure consisting of the sheaf of germs of smooth sections.
\diamond

2.4.
Connections and Curvature

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\pi:{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field with a smooth structure.

i.

A
connection
for
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is a map

\nabla:\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T_{B}))\times\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))\in(\xi,{\mathfrak{f}})\mapsto\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))

such that

\nabla_{f\xi+\eta}=f\nabla_{\xi}+\nabla_{\eta}\quad\text{ and }\nabla_{\xi}(f{\mathfrak{f}})=(\xi f){\mathfrak{f}}+f\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}}

for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(B)
,
\xi\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T_{B}))
and
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
.

ii.

The map
\Theta(\nabla):\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{2}(T_{B})))\times\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))\to\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
defined by

\left<\Theta(\nabla),(\xi\wedge\eta,{\mathfrak{f}})\right>:=\nabla_{\xi}\nabla_{\eta}{\mathfrak{f}}-\nabla_{\eta}\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}}-\nabla_{[\xi,\eta]}{\mathfrak{f}}

is called the
curvature
of
\nabla
.

\diamond

Definition 2.9
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field with smooth structure, and let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a smooth metric for
{\mathfrak{H}}
. A connection
\nabla
for
{\mathfrak{H}}
is said to be
compatible with the metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
if

\left<d{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}),\xi\right>={\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

for all
t\in B
, all
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
and all
\xi\in T_{B,t}
.

\diamond

One defines the covariant derivative of a smooth metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
by

(6)

\nabla{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}):=d({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\nabla{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}).

In terms of this derivative, a connection
\nabla
is compatible with a smooth metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
if and only if
\nabla{\mathfrak{h}}=0
.

The difference of two connections and the curvature of a connection are
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear. If the connections are metric compatible then one can say more.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field with smooth structure, let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a smooth metric for
{\mathfrak{H}}
and let
\nabla,\nabla_{1},\nabla_{2}
be
{\mathfrak{h}}
-compatible connections. Then

\nabla_{2}-\nabla_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta(\nabla)

are tensorial.

Proof.

Denoting either
\nabla_{2}-\nabla_{1}
or
\Theta(\nabla)
by
\mathbf{L}
, one has
{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{g}})
. Therefore if
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)=0
then
{\mathfrak{h}}((\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}})(t),{\mathfrak{g}}(t))=0
for all
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
. Since
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
is dense in
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
,
(\mathbf{L}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0
, i.e.,
\mathbf{L}
is tensorial.
∎

2.5.
Formulation with differential forms

Though it’s largely a matter of taste when the base manifold
B
is finite-dimensional (which is a standing assumption in the present article), one might sometimes prefer to think of the connection as a total differential, rather than talk about directional derivatives. To do so it is convenient to introduce differential forms with values in
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a smooth quasi-Hilbert field. An
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued differential
k
-form is a section
{\mathfrak{b}}
of
\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
. If
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is a smooth quasi-Hilbert field then we say that
{\mathfrak{b}}
is
smooth
if for each
k
-tuple of vector fields
\tau_{1},...,\tau_{k}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T_{B}))
the section
\left<\alpha,\tau_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\tau_{k}\right>
of
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
obtained by contracting in the factor
\Lambda^{k}_{B}
lies in
\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
. We denote by
\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))
the collection of smooth
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
k
-forms.

Remark 2.12
.

It may very well be that the fibers of
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
are infinite-dimensional. In this case, one often sees the analytic completion
\hat{\otimes}
. Here we assume our
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
k
-forms can be written locally as the form
\alpha^{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}+\cdots+\alpha^{m_{k}}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{m_{k}}
, where
m=\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}B
and
m_{k}=\binom{m}{k}
. The
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
k
-form is smooth precisely when the local forms
\alpha^{i}
and the local sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}
are smooth.
\diamond

Remark 2.13
.

Note that since the set of smooth sections restricts to dense subsets of the fibers, the same is automatically true of the set of smooth forms.

\diamond

From the ‘forms’ point of view a connection
\nabla
can be seen as a map

\nabla:\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))\to\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})),

and we can extend
\nabla
to higher twisted exterior derivatives

\nabla:\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))\to\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{k+1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))

in the usual way. A connection is not
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(B)
-linear, but the difference of two connections is. And if the difference
\nabla_{2}-\nabla_{1}
is also tensorial then

\nabla_{2}-\nabla_{1}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{H}}))).

Similarly, if the curvature
\nabla\nabla
of a connection
\nabla
is tensorial then it can be identified with a
2
-form with values in
{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{H}})
:

\Theta(\nabla)\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{2}_{B}\otimes{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{H}}))).

As noted in Proposition
2.10
, this is the case for metric connections.

2.6.
Smooth metrics

Definition 2.14
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field.

i.

A metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
for
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is an assignment to each fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
of a complete inner product
{\mathfrak{h}}_{t}
so that
({\mathfrak{H}}_{t},{\mathfrak{h}}_{t})
and
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
are quasi-isometric for all
t\in B
.

ii.

If
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to B
is a smooth structure for
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
then the metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
is
smooth
if

{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(U)

for any smooth sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
.

\diamond

2.7.
Smooth (quasi-)Hilbert fields

Definition 2.15
.

(1)

A
smooth quasi-Hilbert field
is a quasi-Hilbert field with a smooth structure that admits a connection.

(2)

A smooth quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
over
B
is said to be a
smooth Hilbert field
if it has a connection
\mathring{\nabla}
, called a
reference connection
, and a smooth metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
with the following property. For each
t\in B
,
\xi\in T_{B,t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
there is a constant
C=C(t,\xi,{\mathfrak{f}})
such that

(7)

\left|(\mathring{\nabla}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}})({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))(t)\right|^{2}\leq C{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}})(t).

for each
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
.

\diamond

Proposition 2.16
.

A smooth Hilbert field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}})
has a metric compatible connection.

Proof.

Let
\mathring{\nabla}
be a reference connection. Any other connection is related to
\mathring{\nabla}
by some
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear map of sheaves
\mathbf{A}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T^{*}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})
, i.e., it is of the form
\nabla^{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathring{\nabla}+\mathbf{A}
. To find a metric compatible connection, one needs to find
\mathbf{A}
satisfying
\nabla^{\mathbf{A}}{\mathfrak{h}}=0
(c.f. definition (
6
)), or equivalently

\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})+{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{g}}).

First, by (
7
) we see that for each
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
there exists
\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T^{*}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
such that

\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}).

Since
\overline{\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})}=\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})
, we see that if
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
then

\left|(\mathring{\nabla}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}})({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))(t)\right|^{2}\lesssim{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{f}})(t)

for every
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\rm Domain}(\mathbf{A})
. Thus
{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\rm Domain}(\mathbf{A}^{\dagger})
, the Hilbert space adjoint of
\mathbf{A}
, and moreover

{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{A}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{g}})

for all
{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
. And we also have

{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})}=\tfrac{1}{2}\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})}={\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{g}}),

so that in fact
\mathbf{A}
is self-adjoint on its domain
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
. Therefore

{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})+{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}(2\mathbf{A}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}),

and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

Note that, conversely, the estimates (
7
) on
\mathring{\nabla}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
are necessary for the existence of
\mathbf{A}
. Moreover, the
{\mathfrak{h}}
-Hermitian operator
\mathbf{A}
must be tensorial. There may be other solutions; but all of them must be of the form
\mathbf{A}+\mathbf{B}
such that
\mathbf{B}^{\dagger}=-\mathbf{B}
, with appropriately defined domains which we will not discuss here.

\diamond

2.8.
Holomorphic Quasi-Hilbert fields and BLS fields

Since we are interested in the situation in which the base is a complex manifold, we consider the obvious complexification of the notion of connection: In Definition
2.8
one allows the functions
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(B)
to be complex valued, and for a complex vector field
\xi\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T_{B}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}))
one defines

\nabla_{\xi}:=\nabla_{{\rm Re\ }\xi}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\nabla_{{\rm Im\ }\xi}.

These notions, which just use the natural complex structures of the targets, only require
B
to be a smooth manifold. But if
B
is a complex manifold (or more generally an almost complex manifold) then there is a splitting
T_{B}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}=T^{1,0}_{B}\oplus T^{0,1}_{B}
. Thus one has a decomposition

\nabla=\nabla^{1,0}+\nabla^{0,1}

induced by the splitting

\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{k+1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))=\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{1,0}_{B}\wedge\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))\oplus\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{0,1}_{B}\wedge\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})),

and one can talk about
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
(p,q)
-forms as sections of
\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
. Such forms are smooth if they are smooth as
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
(p+q)
-forms. For
0\leq p,q\leq\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}B
the set of smooth
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued
(p,q)
-forms
\Gamma(B,\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})\cap\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p+q}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))
shall be denoted
\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}))
.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
B
be an almost complex manifold and let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a smooth quasi-Hilbert field.

i.

An almost complex structure for
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is a family of linear maps of sheaves

\bar{\partial}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p,q+1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}),\quad 0\leq p,q\leq n,

compatible with the
\bar{\partial}
-operator on
B
in the sense that

\bar{\partial}(f\wedge{\mathfrak{b}})=\bar{\partial}f\wedge{\mathfrak{b}}+(-1)^{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}f\wedge\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{b}},\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}_{B}).

The almost complex structure is
involutive
or
complex
or
integrable
if
\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}=0
.

ii.

A smooth section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of the smooth quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
with almost complex structure
\bar{\partial}
is said to be
holomorphic
on an open subset
U\subset B
if
\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{f}}=0
on
U
. The set of holomorphic sections on
U
is denoted
\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{O}}({\mathfrak{H}}))
.

iii.

An
(almost) holomorphic quasi-Hilbert field
1
1

1

Since we are now working with complex connections, when we talk about a smooth Hilbert field, in Definition
2.15
.b we replace the vectors
\xi\in T_{B,t}
with vectors vectors
\xi\in T_{B,t}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
.
is a smooth quasi-Hilbert field with an (almost) complex structure.

iv.

A
Berndtsson-Lempert-Szőke
(BLS) field is an almost holomorphic smooth Hilbert field admitting a reference connection
\mathring{\nabla}
such that
\mathring{\nabla}^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}
.

v.

A BLS field is said to be
integrable
if its almost complex structure is integrable.

Remark 2.19
.

If
{\mathfrak{H}}
is an almost holomorphic smooth Hilbert field then
{\mathfrak{H}}
is BLS if and only if for any reference connection
\mathring{\nabla}
the map of sheaves
\mathring{\nabla}^{0,1}-\bar{\partial}
is tensorial and the associated map of Hilbert fields is a morphism.

\diamond

Proposition 2.20
.

On every BLS field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}},\bar{\partial})\to B
there exists a unique connection
\nabla
, called the
BLS Chern connection
, or simply the
Chern connection
, such that

\nabla{\mathfrak{h}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}.

Proof.

Let
\mathring{\nabla}=\mathring{\nabla}^{1,0}+\bar{\partial}
be the reference connection of the underlying quasi-Hilbert field. We need to find a linear map of sheaves
\mathbf{A}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T^{*1,0}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})
such that the connection
\nabla^{\mathbf{A}}:=\mathring{\nabla}+\mathbf{A}
is metric compatible, i.e., satisfies
\nabla_{\mathbf{A}}{\mathfrak{h}}=0
. Our assumption is that for each
\xi\in T_{B,t}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
and each
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
the linear functional
(\mathring{\nabla}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}})({\mathfrak{f}},\cdot)
is bounded. In particular, if we take
\xi\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
, we find that the linear functional
(\mathring{\nabla}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}})({\mathfrak{f}},\cdot)
defined by

(\mathring{\nabla}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}})({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=\partial_{\xi}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathring{\nabla}^{1,0}_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}}{\mathfrak{g}})

is bounded. Hence there exists a linear map of sheaves
\mathbf{A}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(T^{*1,0}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}})
such that

\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathring{\nabla}^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}(\left<\mathbf{A},\zeta\right>{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})\quad\text{ for all }\zeta\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}.

Letting
\nabla:=\mathring{\nabla}^{1,0}+\mathbf{A}+\bar{\partial}
, for
T_{B,t}\ni\xi=\eta+\bar{\eta}\in T_{B,t}^{1,0}\oplus T_{B,t}^{0,1}=T_{B,t}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
and
{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
we compute that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\xi}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\nabla_{\xi}{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))+\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\nabla^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{g}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{g}})+\overline{\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{f}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathring{\nabla}^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{g}})-{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\left<\mathbf{A},\zeta\right>{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\overline{\partial_{\zeta}({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{1,0}_{\zeta}{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{g}},\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\zeta}}{\mathfrak{f}})-{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\left<\mathbf{A},\zeta\right>{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}}\right)}

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore
\nabla:=\mathring{\nabla}+\mathbf{A}
is the Chern connection. Moreover, we have shown that

(8)

{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{1,0}{\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})=\partial({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}))-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})

on all smooth
{\mathfrak{H}}
-valued forms. Since the right hand side does not depend on the connection, we see that the Chern connection is unique.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

In view of the uniqueness of the Chern connection, we shall often refer to the BLS field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}})
and omit explicit reference to the connection. Often we even omit reference to the metric when the latter is clear from the context.

\diamond

Proposition 2.22
.

The curvature
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
of the Chern connection of a BLS field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}},\bar{\partial})\to B
is a smooth
{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{H}})
-valued
2
-form that is
{\mathfrak{h}}
-anti-Hermitian. Moreover, if
\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}=0
then the curvature is a
{\rm Lin}({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{H}})
-valued
(1,1)
-form.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.10

\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
is tensorial. If
{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}
are smooth
k
-forms then

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dd{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})=d\left({\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})+(-1)^{k}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta({\mathfrak{h}}){\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})+(-1)^{k+1}{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})+(-1)^{k}{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\nabla{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}}){\mathfrak{b}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta({\mathfrak{h}}){\mathfrak{b}}_{1},{\mathfrak{b}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}}){\mathfrak{b}}_{2}),

so every metric-compatible connection has anti-Hermitian curvature. In particular, the
(0,2)
-component of a metric-compatible connection is the Hermitian conjugate of the
(2,0)
-component, so if also
\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}=0
then
\nabla^{1,0}\nabla^{1,0}=0
.
∎

2.9.
Smooth (Quasi-)Hilbert Subfields

Definition 2.23
.

Let
({\mathfrak{L}},{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}})\to B
be a smooth Hilbert field.

i.

A smooth Hilbert subfield of
({\mathfrak{L}},{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}})
is a smooth Hilbert field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{H}}})
such that

a)

{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathfrak{L}}_{t}
for every
t\in B
,

b)

{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})=\{{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}}))\ ;\ {\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}\text{ for all }t\in{\rm Domain}({\mathfrak{f}})\}
, and

c)

{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}={\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
.

ii.

To any smooth Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
there is associated the
orthogonal projector
: the unique morphism
P=P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}:{\mathfrak{L}}\to{\mathfrak{H}}
defined by fiberwise orthogonal projection.

iii.

We say that the smooth Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is
regular
if
P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}:{\mathfrak{L}}\to{\mathfrak{H}}
is smooth.

Remark 2.24
.

A smooth quasi-Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a smooth quasi-Hilbert field that satisfies (i.a) and (i.b) of Definition
2.23
.

\diamond

Remark 2.25
.

To any smooth Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
one can associate its orthogonal complement
{\mathfrak{H}}^{\perp}
. In general
{\mathfrak{H}}^{\perp}
need not be a smooth Hilbert subfield of
{\mathfrak{L}}
, but this is certainly the case if
{\mathfrak{H}}
is regular.

\diamond

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
be a smooth (quasi-)Hilbert and
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
a smooth (quasi-)Hilbert subfield. Suppose both fields are equipped with connections
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
and
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
. One can consider the map

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}:=\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}-\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{L}}),

which depends of course on the choice of connections. One computes that

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(f{\mathfrak{f}})=\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}(f{\mathfrak{f}})-\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(f{\mathfrak{f}})=df\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}+f\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}}-(df\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}+f\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}})=f\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}\quad\text{ for all }{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}),

which shows that
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is a linear map of sheaves.

Definition 2.26
.

The linear map of sheaves
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is called the
second fundamental map
of the (quasi)-Hilbert fields with connections.

Proposition 2.27
.

If the connections
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
and
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
are metric-compatible then the map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is tensorial.

Proof.

If
{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)=0
then

{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}((\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}-\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}){\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}-\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}){\mathfrak{g}}),

and the right hand side vanishes at
t
. Therefore by density
(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0
.
∎

Therefore one has a densely defined map of Hilbert fields
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}:{\mathfrak{H}}\to\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{L}}
such that

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}=\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}\quad\text{for all }{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}).

Definition 2.28
.

The map of Hilbert fields
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is called the
second fundamental form
of the Hilbert fields with metric-compatible connections
({\mathfrak{L}},\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})
and
({\mathfrak{H}},\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}})
.

If
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a regular Hilbert subfield then any connection
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
for
{\mathfrak{L}}
defines a connection
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}:=P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
. In this case,
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}=P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}^{\perp}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
. And if
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
is metric-compatible then for any
{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
one has

{\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=d({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{g}})=d({\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}))-{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{g}}),

so that
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
is also metric compatible. Therefore
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is tensorial, and one has a second fundamental form
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
, which moreover takes its values in
\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}^{\perp}
.

Remark 2.29
.

In the case of our main examples
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}
discussed in the introduction, if the Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}
is smooth then it is a regular smooth Hilbert subfield of
{\mathscr{L}}
. This fact is a special case of a result of Kodaira and Spencer, to the effect that smoothness of the harmonic projections is equivalent to constancy of the dimensions of the harmonic spaces. The latter in turn means that the harmonic spaces fit together to form a vector bundle, and in the case of harmonic
(p,0)
-forms, which are automatically holomorphic, this vector bundle is even holomorphic. Thus for
{\mathscr{H}}
, which is the special case
p=n
, Kodaira-Spencer tells us that if
{\mathscr{H}}
is smooth then
{\mathscr{H}}
is a holomorphic vector bundle, and then the projection
P_{{\mathscr{H}}}
is smooth.

\diamond

2.10.
BLS Subfields and the Gauss-Griffiths Formula

Definition 2.30
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B,\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
be an almost holomorphic quasi-Hilbert field.

i.

An almost holomorphic quasi-Hilbert field
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B,\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
is said to be a
subfield
of
{\mathfrak{L}}
if

a)

{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathfrak{L}}_{t}
for every
t\in B
,

b)

{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})=\{{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}}))\ ;\ {\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}\text{ for all }t\in{\rm Domain}({\mathfrak{f}})\}
, and

c)

\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})}=\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}
.

ii.

A subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
of
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
is said to be a
BLS subfield
of
{\mathfrak{L}}
if
P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})\subset{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
.

Remark
.

The inclusions
P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})\subset{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})\subset{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})
imply that
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a regular subfield and that
P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})={\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
. In particular, a smooth section of
{\mathfrak{L}}
that takes all its values in
{\mathfrak{H}}
is a smooth section of
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Conversely, if
{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS field and
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a regular subfield such that the subspace
\{(P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}})(p)\ ;\ {\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})_{p}\}\subset{\mathfrak{H}}_{p}
is dense for every
p\in B
then
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS subfield.

\diamond

Proposition 2.31
.

If
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS subfield then the BLS Chern connection for
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
is
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}}=P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}
, where
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}
denotes the restriction of the connection
\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}
to sections of
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Proof.

If
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathfrak{H}})
then

\displaystyle\partial{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}(P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}(P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}),

and since (
8
) uniquely determines the Chern connection, the result follows.
∎

By property (c) of Definition
2.30
one also sees that

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}=P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}.

Consequently the second fundamental map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
of a BLS subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is tensorial, and its associated second fundamental form
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
takes values in
\Lambda^{1,0}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}^{\perp}
.

Proposition 2.32

(Gauss-Griffiths Formula)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS field and let
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS subfield.

a.

For any smooth sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
and any
(1,0)
-vectors
\xi,\eta\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
,
t\in B
one has

(9)

{\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})(\xi,\bar{\eta}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}})(\xi,\bar{\eta}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta){\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

b.

If moreover the quadratic form

{\mathbb{G}}_{\xi\bar{\eta}}(f_{1},f_{2}):={\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)f_{2})

defines a bounded conjugate-linear functional
{\mathbb{G}}_{\xi,\bar{\eta}}(f,\cdot)
for every
t\in B
, every
f\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
and every pair of
(1,0)
-vectors
\xi,\eta\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
, then

(10)

\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}=\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}})-(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}.

Proof.

Fix
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}}))
. Then by orthogonality

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}\left(((P_{\mathfrak{H}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}+P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0})\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}(P_{\mathfrak{H}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}+P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0})){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}((\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}1,0}\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}1,0}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{h}}((\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}1,0}\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{H}}1,0}){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})+{\mathfrak{h}}(\bar{\partial}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}),{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}).

But

\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}(\bar{\partial}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}),{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}(\bar{\partial}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}),{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}^{\perp}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}),

and since
{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})=0
,

{\mathfrak{h}}(\bar{\partial}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}).

Thus Formula (
9
) is proved.

To obtain Formula (
10
) from Formula (
9
) we need to show that the domain of the Hilbert space adjoint of the densely defined operator
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)
contains the image of
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)
. Now, the domain of
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)
consists of all
\gamma\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}^{\perp}
such that

\left|{\mathfrak{h}}(\gamma,\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)g)\right|^{2}\leq C_{\gamma,\eta}{\mathfrak{h}}(g,g)\quad\text{for all }g\in({\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t})(t).

Since by hypothesis

\left|{\mathbb{G}}_{\xi\bar{\eta}}(f,g)\right|^{2}=\left|{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f,\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)g)\right|^{2}\lesssim{\mathfrak{h}}(g,g),

we see that for each
f\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}

\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f\in{\rm Domain}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)^{\dagger})\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)^{\dagger}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f,g)={\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f,\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)g).

Therefore for each
f\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}

\left<(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}f,\xi\wedge\bar{\eta}\right>=-\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\eta)^{\dagger}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}(\xi)f,

which shows that (
9
) implies (
10
). The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

Note that if
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
has an
integrable
structure, i.e.,
\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}=0
, then the right hand side of (
10
) is a
(1,1)
-form. Thus, even if
\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}\neq 0
, the restriction of the curvature of
{\mathfrak{L}}
to an integrable BLS subbundle is a
(1,1)
-form.

In general, the complex structure of
{\mathfrak{H}}
need not be integrable. In that case, Proposition
2.32
computes only the
(1,1)
-part of the curvature operator of
{\mathfrak{H}}
. It is not hard to extend Proposition
2.32
to compute the full curvature form of
{\mathfrak{H}}
. The part of the formula coming from the form
{\mathbb{G}}
remains of type
(1,1)
, and the type
(2,0)
and
(0,2)
parts come from the curvature form of
{\mathfrak{L}}
.

In our application, in which
{\mathfrak{H}}
is the Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}
defined in the introduction, the complex structure of the subfield is always integrable.

\diamond

Definition 2.34
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS field and let
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS subfield.

a.

The quadratic form
{\mathbb{G}}
defined in Proposition
2.32
.b is called the
Gauss-Griffiths form
of the BLS subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
.

b.

If the Gauss form satisfies the continuity condition of Proposition
2.32
.b then we say that
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a
strongly BLS subfield
.

2.11.
iBLS Fields

If
{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS field and
{\mathfrak{H}}
is a BLS subfield then Formula (
10
) is an identity of Hilbert field maps. Equivalently, Formula (
9
) depends only on the value of the sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}
at a point, and not on higher derivatives of these sections, provided of course that
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
. Similarly, the second fundamental form of a BLS subfield is a pointwise object.

If
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
is a BLS field and
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a Hilbert subfield that is not necessarily BLS, one might try to use the Gauss-Griffiths Formula (
10
) to define the curvature of
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
. There are several issues that arise:

1.

For the resulting curvature of
{\mathfrak{H}}
to be a densely-defined operator, one must know that the set of germs of smooth sections of
{\mathfrak{L}}
at
t\in B
whose value at
t
lies in
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
is dense in
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
.

2.

One must define the second fundamental form of
{\mathfrak{H}}
in
{\mathfrak{L}}
in a manner that does not require a connection for
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

3.

If one wishes to use Formula (
10
) to define the curvature of
{\mathfrak{H}}
as a tensorial operator then the second fundamental form defined in 2 must be tensorial, as must the wedge product of the second fundamental form and its Hilbert space adjoint, i.e., the object corresponding to the second quantity on the right hand side of (
2.32
) must be well-defined.

4.

Ideally the curvature of
{\mathfrak{H}}
should be independent of the ambient BLS field
{\mathfrak{L}}
.

We shall introduce a geometric theory of iBLS fields that resolves the first three issues. Unfortunately, we are unable at present to resolve the fourth issue at the abstract level. However, in the concrete setting of Berndtsson’s iBLS subfield
{\mathscr{H}}
, the large collection of ambient BLS fields
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
that we define (in fact, we could not come up with any other examples of ambient BLS fields for
{\mathscr{H}}
) all yield the same curvature operator.

Substalk bundles

For each
t\in B
let

(11)

\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}:=\{{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})_{t}\text{ and }{\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}\}\subset{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})_{t}.

The space
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector subspace of the stalk
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})_{t}
. Define the family
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}\to B
whose fiber over
t
is
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
.

Remark
.

The ‘family’ notation is only introduced for convenience; we give this family no additional structure. In particular, we note that
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
is not a sheaf, since in general there is no notion of a smooth local section of
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
.

However, we
can
define the notion of a smooth section of
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
over
U\subset B
; it is a section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}}))
such that
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
for all
t\in U
.

\diamond

Definition 2.35
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
be a smooth quasi-Hilbert field. We say that
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a
bona fide
quasi-Hilbert subfield if for each
t\in B
the space
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}(t):=\{{\mathfrak{f}}(t)\ ;\ {\mathfrak{f}}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}\}
is a dense subspace of
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
.

Bona fide subfields resolve Issue 1 above.

Definition 2.36
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
be a BLS field and let
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be a (not necessarily smooth) Hilbert subfield.

(1)

A substalk bundle
{\mathcal{S}}
of
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})
is a choice of
{\mathbb{C}}
-vector subspace
{\mathcal{S}}_{t}\subset{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})_{t}
for each
t\in B
; we write
{\mathcal{S}}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
. We say that
{\mathcal{S}}
is a
tuning
of
{\mathfrak{H}}
(in
{\mathfrak{L}}
), or that
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a
tuned
Hilbert subfield.

(2)

The family
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}\to B
of subspaces of the stalks of
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{L}})
defined by (
11
) is called the maximal substalk bundle of
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

(3)

A substalk bundle
\Sigma\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
is said to
formalize
{\mathfrak{H}}
if

(a)

\Sigma_{t}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
is a dense subspace for every
t
, and

(b)

for every
t\in B
, if
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Sigma_{t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)=0
then
(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
.

In this case we say that
\Sigma
is a
formal
tuning, or that
({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is formally tuned.

Example 2.37
.

If
{\mathfrak{H}}
is a BLS subfield of a BLS field
{\mathfrak{L}}
then the tuning
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
of
{\mathfrak{H}}
is formal. In fact, Property i of Definition
2.36
.c holds because density is part of the BLS hypothesis, and the proof of Proposition
2.27
gives Property ii.

\diamond

As Example
2.37
suggests, tunings of
{\mathfrak{H}}
in
{\mathfrak{L}}
are meant to be ‘non-integrable’ replacements for the non-existent smooth structure
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
of
{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Infinitesimal Second Fundamental Form

Definition 2.38
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS field and let
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})
be a tuned quasi-Hilbert subfield. The map

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})}:=P_{{\mathfrak{H}}}^{\perp}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}

is called the second fundamental map (of
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})
in
{\mathfrak{L}}
).

In some sense,
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})}
is
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B}
-linear. Indeed, for
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B,t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathcal{S}}
one has

P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla(f{\mathfrak{f}})=P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}(df\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}+f\nabla{\mathfrak{f}})=fP^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla{\mathfrak{f}}.

However, the projection
P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}
need not be smooth, so one has to understand this linearity in an appropriate sense.

Obviously
(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}})}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}^{\perp}
. However,
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
is
never
a formal tuning of
{\mathfrak{H}}
, so the map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}})}
is never tensorial
2
2

2

We are slightly abusing language here, since there is no sheaf
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
whose stalk at every
t\in B
is
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
. However,
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
is a
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B,t}
-module, so
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}
-linear means
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{B,t}
-linear, and by
tensorial
we mean that if
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)=0
then
(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0
.
; not even if
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS subfield. The latter claim might seem confusing, in view of Proposition
2.27
. However, the second fundamental map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
in Proposition
2.27
corresponds here to the map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{h}}))}
, and there is no analogue of the sheaf
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})
in the present context, since we are not assuming that
{\mathfrak{H}}
is a smooth quasi-Hilbert field.

Proposition 2.39
.

If
{\mathfrak{L}}
is a BLS field and
({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is a formally tuned Hilbert subfield then there exists a
(1,0)
-form
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}
with values in
{\rm Lin}(\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}},{\mathfrak{H}}^{\perp})
such that

(12)

(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(t){\mathfrak{f}}(t)

for all
t\in B
and all
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Sigma_{t}
.

Proof.

The formality of
\Sigma
implies that the linear map of sheaves
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}
is formal, and thus
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(t)
is well-defined by (
12
).
∎

Remark 2.40
.

The name
formal
is thus revealed to be a play on words: if
({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is formal then its second fundamental map
\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}
is tensorial, and hence defined by a
form
.

\diamond

Infinitesimal (almost) holomorphic subfield

Definition 2.41
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
be a BLS field, let
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be a quasi-Hilbert subfield and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be a tuning of
{\mathfrak{H}}
, i.e., a substalk bundle of
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
. We say that
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})
is an
infinitesimally almost holomorphic subfield
of
{\mathfrak{L}}
if for each
t\in B
, each
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
and each
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{t}
one has

\left<\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\tau}\right>(t)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}.

If moreover
