\left<\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}},\bar{\sigma}\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>(t)=0

for each
t\in B
, all
\sigma,\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
and all
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{t}
then we say that
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})
is an
infinitesimally holomorphic subfield
of
{\mathfrak{L}}
.

Remark 2.42
.

In practice, given a Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}
of a BLS field
{\mathfrak{L}}
, one finds that there are always sections
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}
such that
(\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{L}}}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)\not\in T^{0,1}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
. Thus any useful notion of almost holomorphic subfield must involve a choice of tuning.

\diamond

If the quasi-Hilbert subfield
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is moreover infinitesimally almost holomorphic, which is the case of primary interest for us, then one trivially has the following proposition.

Proposition 2.43
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS field and let
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be an infinitesimally almost holomorphic subfield. Then

\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathcal{S}})}=P^{\perp}_{{\mathfrak{H}}}\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}1,0}|_{{\mathcal{S}}}.

iBLS fields

Propositions
2.39
and
2.43
take care of the first aspect of issue number 3 discussed on page
3.
. The second issue, namely that of showing that
(\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{N}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
is well-defined and tensorial, is better handled by considering the quadratic form (
10
) rather than the formula (
9
) in Proposition
2.32
.

Definition 2.44
.

Let
{\mathfrak{L}}
be a BLS field, let
{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
be a bona fide quasi-Hilbert subfield and let
\Sigma
be a formal tuning of
{\mathfrak{H}}
. The
Gauss-Griffiths form
of
({\mathfrak{L}},{\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is the sesquilinear form
{\mathbb{G}}:\Sigma\times\Sigma\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{2}_{B})
defined by

{\mathbb{G}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}):={\mathfrak{h}}\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{I\!I}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}{\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{\mathbf{I\!I}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}{\mathfrak{g}}\right).

Note that the Gauss-Griffiths form applied to
{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}
is a complex
2
-form, and its contraction with two vectors
\sigma,\tau\in T_{B}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
is given by

{\mathbb{G}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}\left(\mathbf{\mathbf{I\!I}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(\sigma){\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{\mathbf{I\!I}}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(\tau){\mathfrak{g}}\right).

Just as we required the continuity (
7
) of
\mathring{\nabla}{\mathfrak{h}}
in the setting of smooth Hilbert fields in order to guarantee that such Hilbert fields have a metric-compatible connection (c.f. the proof of Proposition
2.16
), if we want the curvature of a bona-fide, formally tuned quasi-Hilbert subfield of a BLS field to be given by a map of sheaves then we must require the analogous continuity of the Gauss-Griffiths form.

Finally, we can define
infinitesimal BLS
fields, or iBLS fields, as follows.

Definition 2.45
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a quasi-Hilbert field.

i.

An iBLS field is a triple
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
where
{\mathfrak{L}}\to B
is a BLS field, called the
ambient
BLS field (for
{\mathfrak{H}}
), and
\Sigma\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}}}
is a substalk bundle, called the
formalizing
substalk bundle, such that

(a)

{\mathfrak{H}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is a bona fide subfield, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathfrak{H}}_{{\mathfrak{L}},t}(t)
is dense in
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
for every
t\in B
,

(b)

({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is an infinitesimally almost holomorphic Hilbert subfield of
{\mathfrak{L}}
, and

(c)

({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)\subset{\mathfrak{L}}
is formally tuned.

ii.

If in addition to (a), (b) and (c) one has that

(d)

for each
t\in B
, every
\sigma,\tau\in T_{B,t}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
and each
f\in\Sigma_{t}(t)
the conjugate-linear functional
{\mathbb{G}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}({\mathfrak{f}}(t),\cdot)_{t}
is bounded on
\Sigma_{t}(t)
then we say that
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
is
strongly iBLS
.

iii.

And if moreover
({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)
is an infinitesimally holomorphic subfield of
{\mathfrak{L}}
then we shall say that
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
is an
integrable
(strongly) iBLS field.

We shall often abuse notation by repressing the triple
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
and referring only to
{\mathfrak{H}}
as an iBLS field when the rest of the data is clear from the context.

Proposition 2.46
.

Let
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
be an iBLS field. Then there exists a unique linear map of substalk bundles
\mathbf{A}:\Sigma\to\Lambda^{2}_{B}\otimes\Sigma
such that

{\mathbb{G}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{h}}\left(\left<\mathbf{A},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>{\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}\right)=-{\mathfrak{h}}\left({\mathfrak{f}},\left<\mathbf{A},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>{\mathfrak{g}}\right).

Proof.

The map
\mathbf{A}
defined by the Riesz Representation Theorem satisfies the first equality. Since
\overline{{\mathbb{G}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})}={\mathbb{G}}_{\tau\bar{\sigma}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})
, we have

{\mathbb{G}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}})=\overline{{\mathbb{G}}_{\tau\bar{\sigma}}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}})}=\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\left<\mathbf{A},\bar{\tau}\wedge\sigma\right>{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}}\right)}=-\overline{{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\left<\mathbf{A},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{f}}\right)}=-{\mathfrak{h}}\left({\mathfrak{f}},\left<\mathbf{A},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>{\mathfrak{g}}\right).

The proof is complete.
∎

In analogy with the Gauss-Griffiths formula, we define the operator
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}
by

\displaystyle\left<\mathbf{A},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\left<(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)},\sigma\wedge\bar{\tau}\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)})^{\dagger},\bar{\tau}\right>\left<\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)},\sigma\right>-\left<\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)},\sigma\right>\left<(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)})^{\dagger},\bar{\tau}\right>.

Definition 2.47
.

The
curvature
of an iBLS field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
is the
2
-form

\Theta^{\text{(rel ${\mathfrak{L}}$)}}({\mathfrak{H}}):=\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}+(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)})^{\dagger}\wedge\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}.

Equivalently, for any
t\in B

{\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta^{\text{(rel ${\mathfrak{L}}$)}}({\mathfrak{H}})(\xi,\eta){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})={\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})(\xi,\eta){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})-{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(\xi){\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}/({\mathfrak{H}},\Sigma)}(\eta){\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

for all
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}
and all
(1,0)
-vectors
\xi,\eta\in{\mathbb{C}}\otimes T_{B_{t}}
.

(We omit reference to
\Sigma
in
\Theta^{(\text{rel ${\mathfrak{L}}$})}
, but the curvature does depend on the choice of
\Sigma
.)

Proposition 2.48
.

If
{\mathfrak{H}}
is an iBLS subfield of a BLS field
{\mathfrak{L}}
and
{\mathfrak{H}}
is integrable, i.e.,
(\bar{\partial}^{2}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0
for every
f\in\Sigma_{t}
, then
\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}
has
(1,1)
-coefficients. More precisely, for each
f_{1},f_{2}\in\Sigma_{t}(t)
and each
\xi,\eta\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
one has
{\mathfrak{h}}\left(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})(\xi,\eta)f_{1},f_{2}\right)={\mathfrak{h}}\left(\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})(\bar{\xi},\bar{\eta})f_{1},f_{2}\right)=0
. Consequently, if
{\mathfrak{H}}
is integrable then
\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{H}})
has
(1,1)
-coefficients, i.e.,

\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{H}})(\xi,\eta)=\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{H}})(\bar{\xi},\bar{\eta})=0

for all
\xi,\eta\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
.

Proof.

The statement about
\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{H}})
follows from the statement about
\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})|_{{\mathfrak{H}}}
and the fact that
\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}/{\mathfrak{H}}}
has
(1,0)
-form coefficients. Finally, since
\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})
is anti-Hermitian, it suffices to show that
\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})^{0,2}{\mathfrak{f}}=0
for any
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathfrak{H}}
. The latter holds because
\Theta(\nabla^{{\mathfrak{L}}})^{0,2}{\mathfrak{f}}=\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}\bar{\partial}^{{\mathfrak{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}=0
for all
{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathfrak{H}}
.
∎

A definition of the curvature of an iBLS subfield that depends heavily on the ambient BLS field is rather undesirable, but we have not found a reasonable way to overcome this issue. On the other hand, the iBLS field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
, which is our main concern, has the interesting property that it has a huge number of natural ambient BLS fields
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
, where
\theta
is the parameter ‘enumerating’ these BLS fields, such that

(i)

{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})
is independent of
\theta
,

(ii)

the formal tuning
\Sigma
of
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is independent of
\theta
, and

(iii)

the curvature
\Theta^{{\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}({\mathscr{H}})
is independent of
\theta
.

In fact, the only dependence on the parameter
\theta
is through the
\bar{\partial}
-operator
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
. We will use these properties to define the curvature of the iBLS field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
.

2.12.
Positivity

All of the definitions of positivity of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles have direct analogues in the setting of BLS fields and iBLS fields, with one important change. Since one wants curvature to measure an infinitesimal quantity related to local variation, it is natural to evaluate the curvature only on vectors that can be continued to a local section. For a BLS field
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}})\to B
the curvature
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
is evaluated at the fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
only on vectors in
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
, while for iBLS fields
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{L}},\Sigma)
over
B
the curvature
\Theta^{(\text{rel }{\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{h}})
is evaluated at the fiber
{\mathfrak{H}}_{t}
only on vectors in
\Sigma_{t}(t)
. For simplicity we shall state the definitions of positivity only for BLS fields. For iBLS fields, simply replace
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
by
\Theta^{(\text{rel }{\mathfrak{L}})}({\mathfrak{H}})
and
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
by
\Sigma_{t}(t)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B,{\mathfrak{h}}
be a BLS field. Using the metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
, one defines Hermitian forms
\{\ ,\ \}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}
on the fibers
T^{1,0}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
by

\left\{\tau_{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\tau_{2}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}:={\mathfrak{h}}(\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})_{\tau_{1},\bar{\tau}_{2}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})

for indecomposable tensors in
T^{1,0}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
, and extending sesqui-linearly on each fiber.

Definition 2.49
.

Let
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
be a BLS field with smooth Hermitian metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
, and fix a smooth Hermitian metric
g
on
B
.

(i)

We say that
{\mathfrak{h}}
has positive curvature in the sense of Griffiths at a point
t\in B
if there exists
c>0
such that

\left\{\tau\otimes{\mathfrak{f}},\tau\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}\right\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}\geq c{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{f}})g(\tau,\tau)

for all
\tau\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
.

(ii)

We say that
{\mathfrak{h}}
has positive curvature in the sense of Nakano at a point
t\in B
if there exists
c>0
such that

\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tau_{j}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{j},\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tau_{k}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{k}\right\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}\geq c\sum_{j=1}^{n}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{j},{\mathfrak{f}}_{j})g(\tau_{j},\tau_{j})

for all
\tau_{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},...,\tau_{n}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{n}\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)
, where
n=\min\left({\rm dim}_{{\mathbb{C}}}B,{\rm Rank}({\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t))\right)
.

(iii)

Let
k
be an integer between
1
and
\min\left({\rm dim}_{{\mathbb{C}}}B,{\rm Rank}({\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t))\right)
. We say that
{\mathfrak{h}}
has
k
-positive curvature at a point
t\in B
if there exists
c>0
such that

\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{k}\tau_{j}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{j},\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\tau_{\ell}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{\ell}\right\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}\geq c\sum_{j=1}^{k}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{j},{\mathfrak{f}}_{j})g(\tau_{j},\tau_{j})

for all
\tau_{1}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},...,\tau_{k}\otimes{\mathfrak{f}}_{k}\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathfrak{H}})_{t}(t)

\diamond

Clearly these notions of positivity do not depend on the choice of the metric
g
. Note furthermore that
1
-positivity is just Griffiths positivity, that
n
-positivity is just Nakano positivity, and that
m
-positivity implies
k
-positivity if
m\geq k
.

There are also corresponding notions of nonnegative curvature (one takes
c
nonnegative) and negative curvature (one reverses the inequalities and takes
c<0
).

3.
Relative Canonical Bundle, Lie Derivatives and Horizontal Lifts

The present section introduces the basic definitions and tools needed to to define our Hilbert fields of interest and to carry out computation of the various geometric objects on these Hilbert fields; objects that were discussed more abstractly in the previous section.

3.1.
Families of twisted canonical sections

Fix a proper holomorphic family of vector bundles
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
. In the Introduction we defined the Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
, whose fibers and metric are respectively

{\mathscr{H}}_{t}:=H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{t}}(K_{X_{t}}\otimes E))\quad\text{and}\quad(f_{1},f_{2})_{t}:=(-1)^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}\int_{X_{t}}\left<f_{1}\wedge\bar{f}_{2},h\right>.

In Section
4
we shall present the iBLS structures for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
. To do so, one must have a way to vary
E
-valued canonical sections on
X_{t}
with respect to
t
. Following Berndtsson, we define the sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
to be fiberwise-holomorphic sections of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
, where
K_{X/B}
denotes the relative canonical bundle. Since
p:X\to B
is a submersion, there are two equivalent ways of defining the relative canonical bundle. The first is as a quotient

K_{X/B}:=K_{X}\otimes p^{*}K_{B}^{*}

of the canonical bundle of
X
by the pullback of the canonical bundle of
B
, and the second is by equivalence classes of
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms.

3.1.1.
Representation of twisted relative canonical sections as twisted canonical sections

Given a point
t\in B
and a coordinate system
s=(s^{1},...,s^{m})
in an open set
U
containing
t
, the restricted projection map
p:X_{U}\to U
, where
X_{U}:=p^{-1}(U)
, can be expressed in terms of coordinates as
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
for global holomorphic functions
p^{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}(X_{U})
. Since
p
is a submersion, i.e.,
dp^{1}\wedge...\wedge dp^{m}
is nowhere-zero on
X_{U}
, any section
F
of
K_{X}|_{X_{t}}\to X_{t}
can be written uniquely as
F=f\wedge dp^{1}\wedge...\wedge dp^{m}
for some section
f
of
K_{X_{t}}\to X_{t}
. This first perspective on the relative canonical bundle is well-suited to extension of canonical sections from a the fiber
X_{t}
to
X
, in the sense of Ohsawa-Takegoshi. But for the purpose of differentiating sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
the ‘canonical sections’ approach has drawbacks. Therefore, we will not be using this point of view except when we discuss the question of local triviality of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
in Section
5
.

3.1.2.
Representation by equivalence classes of relative top-forms

Consider the holomorphic vector bundle
\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\to X
whose local sections are
(n,0)
-forms on
X
, and the subbundle
Z(p)^{n,0}\to X
whose fiber at
x\in X
is defined by

Z(p)^{n,0}_{x}=\left\{f\in\Lambda^{n,0}_{X,x}\ ;\ \left<f,\xi_{1}\wedge...\wedge\xi_{n}\right>=0\text{ for all }\xi_{1}\wedge...\wedge\xi_{n}\in\Lambda^{n}({\rm Kernel}(dp(x)))\right\}.

Thus
Z(p)^{n,0}\to X
is the annihilator of
\Lambda^{n}T^{1,0}_{X/B}
, where
T^{1,0}_{X/B}:={\rm kernel}(dp)
is the so-called
vertical
(1,0)
-tangent bundle
of
p:X\to B
. As
p
is a submersion,
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
is a holomorphic vector bundle of rank
n
, and thus
\Lambda^{n}T^{1,0}_{X/B}
has rank
1
. Therefore there is a line bundle
L
such that

0\to Z(p)^{n,0}\to\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\to L\to 0

is an exact sequence of vector bundles, and the line bundle
L
is precisely
K_{X/B}
. From this perspective a section of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
is an equivalence class of sections of
\Lambda_{X}^{n,0}\otimes E\to X
, where two sections are equivalent if their difference is a section of
Z(p)^{n,0}\otimes E\to X
. We note also that a section
u
of
\Lambda_{X}^{n,0}\otimes E\to X
is a section of
Z(p)^{n,0}\otimes E\to X
if and only if
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=0
for every
t\in B
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a proper holomorphic submersion of complex manifolds. For each
t\in B
there is an isomorphism

\iota_{t}:\Gamma(X_{t},(K_{X/B}\otimes E)|_{X_{t}})\to\Gamma(X_{t},K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}})

such that, with
\iota_{X_{t}}:X_{t}\hookrightarrow X
denoting the natural inclusion of the fiber,

\iota_{t}(f)=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u

for any
(n,0)
-form
u
representing a section
f
of
K_{X/B}\otimes E
over an open subset
U
of
t
.

Remark 3.2
.

In computation one often works locally on the base
B
, in some coordinate chart
U
with local coordinates
s=(s^{1},...,s^{m})
. In terms of these coordinates the projection
p
of the holomorphic family has the expression
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
. The functions
p^{i}
are of course constant on the fibers of
p
, and their differentials
dp^{i}
and
d\bar{p}^{i}
are global forms on
p^{-1}(U)
. Every section of
Z(p)^{n,0}|_{U}
is of the form
dp^{1}\wedge v_{1}+...+dp^{m}\wedge v_{m}
for some smooth
E
-valued
(n-1,0)
-forms
v_{1},...,v_{m}\in\Gamma(p^{-1}(U),{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{X}(\Lambda^{n-1,0}_{X}\otimes E))
. Thus two sections
u_{1},u_{2}
of
\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E|_{U}
define the same
E
-valued relative canonical section over
U
if and only if there exist
v_{1},...,v_{m}\in\Gamma(p^{-1}(U),{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}_{X}(\Lambda^{n-1,0}_{X}\otimes E))
such that

u_{2}-u_{1}=dp^{1}\wedge v_{1}+\cdots+dp^{m}\wedge v_{m},

or equivalently
u_{1}\wedge dp^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dp^{m}=u_{2}\wedge dp^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dp^{m}
.

\diamond

3.2.
Lie derivatives of twisted forms

We shall need to compute derivatives of integrals of the form (
1
). The usual tool for taking the derivative ‘through the integral’ is the Lie derivative. When the objects being differentiated have natural action by diffeomorphisms, the Lie derivative has a very natural definition, namely differentiation of the pullback by the flow of the vector field along which one is computing derivatives. For example, differential forms, vector fields and the tensors one can construct from these behave well with respect to this definition.

In the twisted setting, i.e., when one looks at differential forms with values in a vector bundle, one can still make use of the classical Lie derivative by working in locally trivial open sets, but many formulas become local and keeping track of these local formulas can be cumbersome. It is clearer and more efficient to identify certain combinations of terms with a global meaning, and to work with those combinations of terms as the basic objects.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold, let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with connection
\nabla
, let
u
be an
E
-valued
(p,q)
-form and let
\xi
be a complex vector field on
X
, i.e., a section of
T_{X}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\to X
.

i.

The Lie derivative along
\xi
, which acts on complex-valued forms, is defined by

L_{\xi}:=L_{{\rm Re\ }\xi}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}L_{{\rm Im\ }\xi},

where
L
on the right-hand side denotes the real Lie derivative of real vector fields.

ii.

The twisted Lie derivative of an
E
-valued
(p,q)
-form
u
along
\xi
is

L^{\nabla}_{\xi}u=L_{\xi}u:=\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)+\xi\lrcorner\nabla u,

Remark 3.4
.

In the untwisted case, i.e., when the underlying vector bundle
E
is the trivial line bundle and the connection
\nabla
is the usual exterior derivative, Definition
3.3
.ii agrees with the aforementioned definition of Lie derivative because of the formula

L_{\xi}u=d(\xi\lrcorner u)+\xi\lrcorner(du)

of H. Cartan.

\diamond

Via the splitting
\nabla=\nabla^{1,0}+\nabla^{0,1}
one can define the
(1,0)
- and
(0,1)
-Lie derivative of a
(p,q)
-form
u
with values in a holomorphic vector bundle along a complex vector field
\xi
by

L^{\nabla 1,0}_{\xi}u:=\nabla^{1,0}(\xi\lrcorner u)+\xi\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\quad\text{and}\quad L^{\nabla 0,1}_{\xi}u:=\nabla^{0,1}(\xi\lrcorner u)+\xi\lrcorner\nabla^{0,1}u.

When the connection
\nabla
is clear from the context, we shall write
L^{1,0}_{\xi}
instead of
L^{\nabla 1,0}_{\xi}
(and similarly for
(0,1)
) but we shall retain the notation
L^{\nabla}
for the full twisted Lie derivative for both clarity and emphasis.

Remark
.

Note that
L^{1,0}_{\xi}
(resp.
L^{0,1}_{\xi}
) preserves the bi-degree
(p,q)
if and only if
\xi
is a
(1,0)
(resp.
(0,1)
) vector field or
q=0
(resp.
p=0
).

\diamond

Remark
.

One can also define
L^{0,1}_{\xi}u:=\xi\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u+\bar{\partial}(\xi\lrcorner u)
. In the present article these two meanings of
L^{0,1}_{\xi}
do not conflict because we only use connections
\nabla
satisfying
\nabla^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}
.

\diamond

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
X
be a Kähler manifold of dimension
n+m
, let
Z\subset X
be an
n
-dimensional submanifold, let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with Hermitian metric
h
and its Chern connection, and let
u,u^{\prime}
be
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on
X
. If
\xi
is a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X
then

\iota_{Z}^{*}L_{\xi}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi}u\right)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<u\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}u^{\prime}},h\right>

and

\iota_{Z}^{*}L_{\bar{\xi}}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}u\right)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<u\wedge\overline{L^{1,0}_{\xi}u^{\prime}},h\right>.

Proof.

We compute that

\displaystyle L_{\xi}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\left(\xi\lrcorner\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>\right)+\xi\lrcorner d\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\left(\left<(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+(-1)^{n}\left<u\wedge\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>\right)+\xi\lrcorner\left(\left<\nabla u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+(-1)^{n}\left<u\wedge\overline{\nabla u^{\prime}},h\right>\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+(-1)^{n-1}\left<(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\overline{\nabla u^{\prime}},h\right>+(-1)^{n}\left<\nabla u\wedge\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>

\displaystyle+\left<u\wedge\overline{\nabla(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>+\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+(-1)^{n+1}\left<\nabla u\wedge\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n}\left<\xi\lrcorner u\wedge\overline{\nabla u^{\prime}},h\right>+\left<u,\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla u^{\prime}))},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<u\wedge\overline{\nabla(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>+\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<u,\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla u^{\prime}))},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<u\wedge\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla u^{\prime}))},h\right>,

where the last equality holds because
\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime}=0
. Therefore

\displaystyle\iota_{Z}^{*}L_{\xi}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<u,\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla u^{\prime}))},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<\nabla^{1,0}(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla^{1,0}u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<u,\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}u^{\prime}))},h\right>,

the last equality being a consequence of the fact that upon restriction to
Z
all of the forms whose bidegree is not
(n,n)
must vanish.
∎

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a proper holomorphic family, let
E\to X,h
be a Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle equipped with its Chern connection and let
u
and
u^{\prime}
be
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on
X
. If
\tau
is a
(1,0)
-vector field on
B
and
\xi_{\tau}
is a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X
such that
dp\xi_{\tau}=\tau
then

\tau\int_{X_{t}}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\int_{X_{t}}\left<u\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}_{\tau}}u^{\prime}},h\right>,

where
X_{t}:=p^{-1}(t)
.

Proof.

Write
\tau=\tau_{1}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\tau_{2}
in terms of its real and imaginary parts, and similarly write
\xi_{\tau}=\xi_{1}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\xi_{2}
. Since
p
is holomorphic,
dp\xi_{i}=\tau_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\tau_{i}\int_{X_{t}}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right|_{r=0}\int_{\Phi^{r}_{\xi_{i}}(X_{t})}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right|_{r=0}\int_{X_{t}}\left(\Phi^{r}_{\xi_{i}}\right)^{*}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}L_{\xi_{i}}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>,

so the lemma follows from Definition
3.3
and Proposition
3.5
.
∎

Similarly one has the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
X
,
Z
,
E
and
h
be as in Proposition
3.5
, let
\alpha
and
\beta
be
E
-valued forms of bidegree
(n,1)
and
(n-1,0)
repectively. If
\xi
is a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X
then

L^{1,0}_{\xi}\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>=\left<(L^{1,0}_{\xi}\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}\beta},h\right>,

and hence

\int_{Z}L^{1,0}_{\xi}\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>=\int_{Z}\left<(L^{1,0}_{\xi}\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\int_{Z}\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}\beta},h\right>.

Proof.

We compute that

\displaystyle L^{1,0}_{\xi}\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi\lrcorner\partial\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\partial\left(\xi\lrcorner\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi\lrcorner\left<\nabla^{1,0}\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+(-1)^{n+1}\xi\lrcorner\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{\bar{\partial}\beta},h\right>+\partial\left<\xi\lrcorner\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<(\xi\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+(-1)^{n+1}\left<(\xi\lrcorner\alpha)\wedge\overline{\bar{\partial}\beta},h\right>+\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{\bar{\xi}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}\beta},h\right>

\displaystyle+\left<\nabla^{1,0}(\xi\lrcorner\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+(-1)^{n}\left<\xi\lrcorner\alpha\wedge\overline{\bar{\partial}\beta},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<(\xi\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\left<\nabla^{1,0}(\xi\lrcorner\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{\bar{\xi}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}\beta},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left<(L^{0,1}_{\xi}\alpha)\wedge\bar{\beta},h\right>+\left<\alpha\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}\beta},h\right>,

and the proof is completed by integrating over
Z
.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
X
,
Z
,
E
and
h
be as in Proposition
3.5
, let
u
and
u^{\prime}
be
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on
X
and let
\gamma
be an
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-form on
X
. If
\xi
is a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X
then

(13)

L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}}\left<\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>=\left<L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}}\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>+\left<\gamma\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\xi}u},h\right>

and

(14)

L^{0,1}_{\xi}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\left<(L^{0,1}_{\xi}u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>.

In particular,

(15)

\iota_{Z}^{*}L_{\bar{\xi}}\left<\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>=\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}}\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>+\iota_{Z}^{*}\left<\gamma\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\xi}u},h\right>

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.5
we have

L_{\bar{\xi}}\left<\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>=\left<\nabla(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner\gamma)\wedge\bar{u},h\right>+\left<(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla\gamma))\wedge\bar{u},h\right>+\left<\gamma\wedge\overline{\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)},h\right>+\left<\gamma\wedge\overline{(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))},h\right>

and

L_{\xi}\left<u\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>=\left<\nabla(\xi\lrcorner u)\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<(\xi\lrcorner(\nabla u))\wedge\bar{u}^{\prime},h\right>+\left<u\wedge\overline{\nabla(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner u^{\prime})},h\right>+\left<u\wedge\overline{(\bar{\xi}\lrcorner(\nabla u^{\prime}))},h\right>.

Taking the
(n,n)
-components of both sides of the first identity yields (
13
), and taking the
(n-1,n+1)
-parts of the second identity yields (
14
). Finally, since
L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}}\left<\gamma\wedge\bar{u},h\right>
is of bidegree
(n-1,n+1)
, (
15
) follows from (
13
)
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of Hermitian holomorphic vector bundles, let
\tau
be a holomorphic vector field on
B
, let
\xi_{\tau}
be a lift of
\tau
to
X
, and let
u
be an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form on
X
. Then with respect to the Chern connection of
(E,h)

\bar{\partial}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u\right)=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u-\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner u)+L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u\right),

where the
(n,1)
-form
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}((\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u)
is defined by

\bar{\zeta}\lrcorner\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left((\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u\right)\right):=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi_{\tau}\bar{\zeta}}u\right).

Proof.

One has

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\bar{\partial}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\bar{\partial}\nabla^{1,0}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner u)+\bar{\partial}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\bar{\partial}\nabla^{1,0}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner u)-\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}\nabla^{1,0}u+(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\Theta(h)(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner u)-\nabla^{1,0}\bar{\partial}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner u)-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h)u)+\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner(\nabla^{1,0}\bar{\partial}u)+(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u-\left(\nabla^{1,0}\bar{\partial}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner u)-\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner(\nabla^{1,0}\bar{\partial}u)\right)+(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u-\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner u)+\nabla^{1,0}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u)+\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\bar{\partial}u+(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u-\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner u)+L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u+(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(-(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u-\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner u)+L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u\right).

Indeed,
(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u=0
, because
\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
is vertical, and
\nabla^{1,0}u
has bidegree
(n+1,0)
.
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles, let
t\in B
and let
\tau\in H^{0}(B,{\mathcal{O}}(T^{1,0}_{B}))
. Let
\xi_{\tau}
be a lift of
\tau
, i.e., a
(1,0)
-vector field
\xi_{\tau}
on
X
such that
dp\xi_{\tau}=\tau
. Then with respect to the Chern connection for
(E,h)
, one has the following statements.

a.

If
u,\tilde{u}\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
agree on the fibers of
X\to B
, i.e., they represent the same smooth section
[u]=[\tilde{u}]
of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
, then

\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u=\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\tilde{u}\quad\text{and}\quad\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}\tilde{u}.

b.

If moreover
\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}u\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
then

\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u

for any other lift
\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}
of
\tau
.

c.

Finally, if
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=0
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=0
then
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
.

Proof.

Since
v:=\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}-\xi_{\tau}
is tangent to the fibers of
p
,

\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u_{1}-\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u_{1}=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{v}\bar{\partial}u=L^{1,0}_{v_{t}}\bar{\partial}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=0,

where
v_{t}:=v|_{X_{t}}
. Thus (b) is proved.

To prove (a) it suffices to show that if
u\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
restricts to
0
on fibers then
L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u
and
L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u
restrict to
0
on fibers. Now, using coordinates
s=(s^{1},...,s^{m})
on the base, in terms of which we write
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
and
\tau=\tau^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{\mu}}
(see Remark
3.2
), the section
u
vanishes on fibers if and only if locally in the base one has

u=dp^{\mu}\wedge v_{\mu}

for some
E
-valued
(n-1,0)
-forms
v_{1},...,v_{m}
. But then

L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u=L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}dp^{\mu}\wedge v_{\mu}=(p^{*}\partial\tau^{\mu}\wedge v_{\mu})+dp^{\mu}\wedge\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}v_{\mu}=dp^{\mu}\wedge\left(p^{*}\left(\tfrac{\partial\tau^{\nu}}{\partial s^{\mu}}\right)v_{\nu}+L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}v_{\mu}\right)

and

-L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}dp^{\mu}\wedge\bar{\partial}v_{\mu}=(p^{*}\partial\tau^{\mu}\wedge\bar{\partial}v_{\mu})+dp^{\mu}\wedge\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}v_{\mu}=dp^{\mu}\wedge\left(p^{*}\left(\tfrac{\partial\tau^{\nu}}{\partial s^{\mu}}\right)\bar{\partial}v_{\nu}+L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}v_{\mu}\right),

so that
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u=0
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=0
for all
t\in B
.

Finally, if
\tau
is a holomorphic vector field,
\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
is a
(0,1)
-form with values in the vertical bundle
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
. Therefore if
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=0
then
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\nabla^{1,0}(\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau})\lrcorner u)\right)=0
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u
. If also
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=0
then by Lemma
3.9

\bar{\partial}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}u\right)=0
, so (c) is proved.
∎

3.3.
Horizontal lifts

In differentiating sections sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
or the ambient
L^{2}
bundle
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
one must choose
horizontal
lifts of
(1,0)
-vector fields from
B
to
X
.

Definition 3.11
.

A horizontal distribution for the smooth family
p:X\to B
is a distribution
H\subset T_{X}
such that
dp|_{H}:H\to T_{B}
is an isomorphism of vector bundles. Equivalently, there is a morphism of vector bundles
\theta:T_{B}\to T_{X}
such that
dp\theta(\tau)=\tau
.

\diamond

We shall abuse notation and write
\theta
instead of
H
for the horizontal distribution. Thus
\theta
is both a subbundle of
T_{X}
and a right inverse to
dp:T_{X}\to T_{B}
.

In our situation the total space
X
and base manifold
B
are complex manifolds and
p:X\to B
is holomorphic, i.e.,
dpJ=Jdp
, where
J
denotes the almost complex structures. Regardless of whether
J
is integrable, there is a map that assigns to any horizontal distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
a unique horizontal
(1,0)
-distribution
\theta^{1,0}\subset T^{1,0}_{X}
, defined as
\theta^{1,0}=s^{1,0}\theta
, where

s^{1,0}:T_{X}\ni\xi\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}(\xi-\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}J\xi)\in T^{1,0}_{X}.

The correspondence
\theta\leftrightarrow\theta^{1,0}
is bijective, and since
p:X\to B
is holomorphic,

dp\theta^{1,0}\cong T^{1,0}_{B}\quad\text{and}\quad dp\theta^{0,1}\cong T^{0,1}_{B},

where
\theta^{0,1}:=\overline{\theta^{1,0}}
.

To slightly shrink the sizes of many already-too-large formulas, we shall write

\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}:=\theta^{1,0}(\tau)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}:=\theta^{0,1}(\bar{\tau})=\overline{\theta^{1,0}(\tau)},\qquad\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B}.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a holomorphic submersion and let
\theta
be a horizontal distribution for
p
. Then
\theta
is integrable if and only if

(16)

\left[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\right]=\xi^{\theta}_{[\sigma,\tau]}

for all local vector fields
\sigma,\tau
on
B
.

Proof.

If (
16
) holds then the Lie brackets of vector fields tangent to
\theta
are tangent to
\theta
, i.e.,
\theta
is integrable.

Conversely, suppose
\theta
is integrable. Then
\left[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\right]=\xi^{\theta}_{\eta}
for some vector field
\eta
on
B
, and we must show that
\eta=[\sigma,\tau]
. By the Inverse Function Theorem each point
P\in X
has a local coordinate system
(t,x)
such that
p(t,x)=t
is a local coordinate at
p(P)
. In these coordinates, if
\sigma=\sigma^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}
and
\tau=\tau^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}}
then

\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}=\sigma^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}+\alpha^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}=\tau^{\nu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}}+\beta^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}

and therefore

\left[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\right]=[\sigma,\theta]^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}+\gamma^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}.

Thus

\eta=dp\left(\left[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\right]\right)=[\sigma,\tau],

as claimed.
∎

4.
Hilbert Fields of Sections

In this section we discuss the structure of the Hilbert fields
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
and
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
associated to a holomorphic family of vector bundles
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
that we defined in the introduction.

4.1.
Berndtsson’s Hilbert field

Definition 4.1
.

The Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
with its
L^{2}
-inner product (
1
) is called the
Berndtsson Hilbert field of the family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
.

\diamond

Smooth and holomorphic sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B

We begin with Berndtsson’s definition of sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
. However, we phrase the definition in terms of sections of the relative canonical bundle, and for the moment repress the fact that these sections are equivalence classes of twisted
(n,0)
-forms.

Definition 4.2
.

A section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a section
f\in\Gamma(X,K_{X/B}\otimes E)
such that for each
t\in B

\iota_{t}f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}=H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathcal{O}}(K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}))
(c.f. Proposition
3.1
). We write

{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{H}}).

We also use the notation

{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}(f)\quad\text{ and }\quad f={\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}),

and say that
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{H}})
is induced by
f
, and that
f\in\Gamma(X,K_{X/B}\otimes E)
is associated to
{\mathfrak{f}}
.
We say
{\mathfrak{f}}:B\to{\mathscr{H}}
is holomorphic (resp.
{\mathscr{C}}^{r}
, measurable , etc.) if
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
is holomorphic (resp.
{\mathscr{C}}^{r}
, measurable , etc.). We denote by
\Gamma(U,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}}))
and
\Gamma(U,{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{H}}))
the set of smooth and holomorphic sections over an open set
U\subset B
. Finally, we let
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}})
and
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{H}})
denote the sheaves of germs of smooth and holomorphic sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
respectively.

\diamond

Remark 4.3
.

We emphasize that there may well exist
t\in B
and
f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
such that there is no
smooth
(or even continuous) section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
satisfying
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)=f
. This is the case precisely when
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is not locally trivial near
t\in B
.

\diamond

Unfortunately the sheaf
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}})
does not always define a smooth structure on
{\mathscr{H}}
. The next proposition characterizes precisely when this is the case.

Proposition 4.4
.

Suppose that the holomorphic family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
is proper, or more generally that all of the fibers of the resulting Berndtsson Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}
are finite-dimensional. Then the sheaf
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}})
is a smooth structure for the Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}
, in the sense of Definition
2.4
.i
, if and only if
{\mathscr{H}}
is a holomorphic vector bundle.

Proof.

If
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is locally trivial then the smooth sections clearly form a dense subset on every fiber; in fact, every element of every fiber is the evaluation of a smooth (and even holomorphic) section. Conversely, if
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
has a smooth structure then
t\mapsto\dim{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
is lower semi-continuous. Indeed, if
t_{o}\in B
then we can choose smooth sections
f_{1},...,f_{N}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}})_{t_{o}}
such that
\{f_{1}(t_{o}),...,f_{N}(t_{o})\}\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{t_{o}}
is a basis, since the smooth sections are dense. By continuity,
\{f_{1}(t),...,f_{N}(t)\}\subset{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
is linearly independent for every
t
in some neighborhood
U
of
t_{o}
. Thus
\dim{\mathscr{H}}_{t}\geq\dim{\mathscr{H}}_{t_{o}}
for all
t\in U
.

On the other hand, it is well-known that
t\mapsto\dim{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
is upper semi-continuous. (One can deduce upper semi-continuity from Grauert’s theorem on the coherence of proper direct images, but in the present setting of a proper holomorphic family this upper semi-continuity is a relatively simple consequence of Montel’s Theorem.) Therefore the rank of the fibers of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is constant, and thus, by a well-known result of Kodaira and Spencer,
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a holomorphic vector bundle.
∎

Berndtsson’s
\bar{\partial}
operator for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B

Let us now turn to the definition of the
\bar{\partial}
operator for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
. We begin with the following simple but important fact.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
u
be an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form on the total space of the family
X\to B
, let
t\in B
, and suppose that
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
. Then for any lifts
\xi^{1}_{\tau}
and
\xi^{2}_{\tau}
of the
(1,0)
-vector
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
one has

\bar{\xi}^{1}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=\bar{\xi}^{2}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u

as
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on
X
along
X_{t}
.

Proof.

The proposition is equivalent to the requirement that
\bar{\eta}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=0
for any
\eta\in T^{1,0}_{X_{t}}
. Since
\eta
is tangent to
X_{t}
,
\bar{\eta}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=0
if and only if
\bar{\eta}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u=0
, and the latter holds because
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u
is holomorphic.
∎

By Proposition
4.5
if
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{H}})
then the associated section
f:={\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
satisfies

(17)

\bar{\partial}f(\bar{\zeta})=0\quad\text{ for all }\zeta\in T^{1,0}_{X/B},

where
T^{1,0}_{X/B}:={\rm kernel}(dp)\cap T^{1,0}_{X}\subset T^{1,0}_{X}
is the vertical holomorphic tangent bundle. One has an exact sequence of anti-holomorphic vector bundles

0\to T^{0,1}_{X/B}\to T^{0,1}_{X}\to p^{*}T^{0,1}_{B}\to 0,

and therefore
\bar{\partial}f
induces a unique section
\bar{\eth}f\in\Gamma(X,p^{*}T^{*0,1}_{B}\otimes K_{X/B}\otimes E)
defined by

\bar{\eth}f((x,\bar{\tau}))=\bar{\partial}f(x)(\bar{\xi}_{\tau})\quad\text{ for any }\xi_{\tau}\in T^{1,0}_{X,x}\text{ such that }dp(x)\xi_{\tau}=\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,p(x)}.

We therefore obtain the following definition, which is equivalent to the definition of
\bar{\partial}
for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
given in
[
B-2009
]
.

Definition 4.6

(
\bar{\partial}
-operator for
{\mathscr{H}}
)
.

The operator

\bar{\partial}:\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}}))\to\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{0,1}_{B}\otimes{\mathscr{H}}))

defined by

\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{f}}:={\mathfrak{i}}(\bar{\eth}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}))

is the
\bar{\partial}
-operator for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
.

Remark 4.7
.

Evidently
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{H}}))
in the sense of Definition
4.2
if and only if
\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{f}}=0
, i.e., if and only if
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathcal{O}}({\mathscr{H}}))
in the sense of Definition
2.18
.

\diamond

Remark 4.8
.

For higher-bidegree forms
{\mathfrak{b}}
, expressed in terms of a
\bar{\partial}
-closed local frame
\alpha^{1},...,\alpha^{N}
for
\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}
as
{\mathfrak{b}}=\alpha^{i}\otimes{\mathfrak{b}}_{i}
, the operator
\bar{\partial}
is given by

\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{b}}=\alpha^{i}\otimes\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{b}}_{i},

and therefore
\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}=0
. Thus
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a holomorphic Hilbert field.

\diamond

Remark 4.9
.

At first glance, the definition of the
\bar{\partial}
operator for
{\mathscr{H}}
appearing in
[
B-2009
]
might look somewhat different than the definition we presented here. Here we have used the representation of sections of
{\mathscr{H}}
as relative canonical sections
f\in p_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(K_{X/B}\otimes E)_{t}
, whereas in Berndtsson’s approach one chooses an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
u\in p_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E)
representing
f
. Since
f
is holomorphic on fibers, the section
\bar{\partial}f
of
\Lambda^{0,1}_{X}\otimes K_{X/B}\otimes E
vanishes on the vertical bundle
T^{0,1}_{X/B}
. In terms of the representative
u
of
f
,
\bar{\partial}u
, which is a section of
\Lambda^{n,1}_{X}\otimes E
, need not vanish on
T^{0,1}_{X/B}
, but the restriction of
\bar{\partial}u
to every fiber of
p
must vanish. Therefore, if
\xi_{\tau}
is any lift of
\tau
to
X
one has

\iota_{t}(\bar{\xi}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}f)=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\xi}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u),

where
\iota_{t}
is the isomorphism of Proposition
3.1
.

In terms of the local coordinates discussed in Remark
3.2
, there exist
(n,0)
-forms
\beta_{1},...,\beta_{m}
and
(n-1,1)
-forms
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m}
such that

\bar{\partial}u=dp^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}+d\bar{p}^{\nu}\wedge\beta_{\bar{\nu}}.

If
\tilde{u}
is another representative of
f
then
\tilde{u}-u=dp^{\mu}\wedge\gamma_{\mu}
for some
(n-1,0)
-forms
\gamma_{1},...,\gamma_{m}
, so

\bar{\partial}\tilde{u}=dp^{\mu}\wedge(\alpha_{\mu}-\bar{\partial}\gamma_{\mu})+d\bar{p}^{\nu}\wedge\beta_{\bar{\nu}}.

Therefore the section
\bar{\eth}f
is

\bar{\eth}f=p^{*}d\bar{s}^{\mu}\otimes[\beta_{\mu}],

where
[\beta]
means the relative canonical form represented by the
(n,0)
-form
\beta
. The right-hand side of the latter identity is Berndtsson’s definition of
\bar{\partial}

[
B-2009
, page 544]
.

\diamond

4.2.
The
\mathbf{L^{2}}
BLS fields

We shall now construct a large collection of BLS fields each of which contains the Berndtsson Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
of the family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
as a subfield, and which makes
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
into an iBLS field. The underlying smooth structure of all of these BLS fields is the same, and it is the complex structure that changes.

The underlying Hilbert field

The fibers of the underlying Hilbert field are the
L^{2}
-spaces

{\mathscr{L}}_{t}=\left\{\text{measurable sections }f\in\Gamma(X_{t},K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}})\ ;\ (-1)^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}\int_{X_{t}}\left<f\wedge\bar{f},h\right><+\infty\right\}.

Of course, the Hermitian metric is just the
L^{2}
-metric.

The smooth structure

Definition 4.10
.

A smooth section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
is a section
f\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(K_{X/B}\otimes E))
. We write
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
.

As with the Berndtsson Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
, we write
f={\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
and
{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}(f)
to relate the sections of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
and the sections of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
.

As a consequence of the general theory of Section
2
, differential
k
-forms and
(p,q)
-forms with values in
{\mathscr{L}}
are sections of
\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}\to B
and
\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}\to B
respectively, and can be identified with sections of
p^{*}\Lambda^{k}_{B}\otimes K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
and
p^{*}\Lambda^{p,q}_{B}\otimes K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
. The discussion is analogous to that of Berndtsson’s Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}
.

It is a basic fact that

(i)

the collection of smooth members of
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
is dense, and

(ii)

every smooth member of
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
has a smooth extension to a section of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
.

It is also obvious that

(iii)

the
L^{2}
metric is smooth with respect to
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}})
.

Thus
{\mathscr{L}}
is a smooth Hilbert field. (In fact, as mentioned in Remark
1.5
, by Ehresmann’s Theorem
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
is even locally trivial, i.e., it is a smooth Hilbert bundle.)

Some reference connections

Let
D
be any connection for
E\to X
. We define the connection
\mathring{\nabla}
for
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
by the formula

(18)

\mathring{\nabla}_{\sigma+\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left(L^{D1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)+{\mathfrak{i}}\left(L^{D0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right),

where
\sigma,\tau=T^{1,0}_{B,t}
.

Proposition 4.11
.

For any connection
D
for
E\to X
the connection
\mathring{\nabla}
for
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
is a reference connection for the
L^{2}
metric, i.e., it satisfies the continuity assumption
(
7
)
of Definition
2.15
.b
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}
denote the
L^{2}
metrics for
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
. A direct computation shows that

\mathring{\nabla}_{\sigma+\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{h}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})=\int_{X}\left<u_{1}\wedge\bar{u}_{2},Dh\right>,

and condition (
7
) obviously holds.
∎

The almost complex structures

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper holomorphic family. For a horizontal distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
and a smooth section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
define

\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}:={\mathfrak{i}}\left(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right),\quad\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B}.

The operator
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
is extended to smooth
{\mathscr{L}}
-valued differential forms in the usual way.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a proper holomorphic submersion and let
\theta\subset T_{X}
be a horizontal distribution. Then the following are equivalent.

a.

The almost complex structure
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
is a complex structure, i.e.,
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}\bar{\partial}^{\theta}=0
.

b.

The distribution
\theta
is integrable.

Proof.

By definition of
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}

\left(\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\tau}}-\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\tau}}\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\bar{\sigma}}}-\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{[\bar{\sigma},\bar{\tau}]}\right){\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left(\left(\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}-\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}}-\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{[\sigma,\tau]}}\right){\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)

for any
(1,0)
-vector fields
\sigma,\tau
on
B
. Now, on any complex manifold one has

\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}_{1}}\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}_{2}}-\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}_{2}}\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}_{1}}-\bar{\partial}_{[\bar{\xi}_{1},\bar{\xi}_{2}]}=0

for any
(1,0)
-vector fields
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
. Since we are acting on sections of a holomorphic vector bundle, the first two terms are always well-defined, and the third term is well-defined because
[\xi_{1},\xi_{2}]
is also a
(1,0)
-vector field. Therefore

\left(\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\tau}}-\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\tau}}\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\bar{\bar{\sigma}}}-\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{[\bar{\sigma},\bar{\tau}]}\right){\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left(\left(\bar{\partial}_{[\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}-\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{[\sigma,\tau]}}\right){\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right),

and the right hand side vanishes on all smooth sections if and only if
[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}]=\xi^{\theta}_{[\sigma,\tau]}
for all smooth
(1,0)
-vector fields
\sigma,\tau
on
B
. In view of Proposition
3.12
the latter holds if and only if
\theta
is integrable.
∎

The
L^{2}
BLS field of
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B

Note that if in Formula (
18
) we take the connection
D
to be complex, i.e., such that
D^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}
, then the resulting reference connection
\mathring{\nabla}
for
{\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
satisfies
\mathring{\nabla}^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
. Therefore all of the properties of a BLS field required by Definition
2.18
.iv are satisfied for
{\mathscr{L}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
.

Definition 4.14
.

The BLS field
({\mathscr{L}},{\mathfrak{h}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})\to B
is called the
L^{2}
BLS field of the family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
associated to the horizontal distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
.

The notation
({\mathscr{L}},{\mathfrak{h}},\bar{\partial}^{\theta})
for the BLS field of the family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
can become cumbersome. Since for a family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
(which is seen as fixed from the outset, and has already been suppressed from the notation) this BLS structure is completely determined by
\theta
, we shall often simply denote it
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
.

The BLS subfield
{\mathscr{H}}
in the regular case

In Paragraph
4.1
we defined the
\bar{\partial}
-operator for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
by

\bar{\partial}_{\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}:={\mathfrak{i}}(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})),\quad\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B},

and observed (Proposition
4.5
) that the right hand side is independent of the choice of lift. Thus we have the following trivial but fundamental observation.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of Hermitian vector bundles. Then for any distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
the operator
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}|_{{\mathscr{H}}}
agrees with the
\bar{\partial}
-operator for
{\mathscr{H}}
given in Definition
4.6
. In particular, if
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is locally trivial then for any horizontal distribution
\theta
the holomorphic Hilbert field
({\mathscr{H}},\bar{\partial})
is a BLS subfield of the BLS field
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
.

4.3.
The iBLS Structures of Berndtsson’s Hilbert Field

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of Hermitian vector bundles, fix
t_{o}\in B
, and let
\theta\subset T_{X}
be any horizontal distribution. Then for any
f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t_{o}}
there exists a smooth section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}))
such that if
u\in\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
represents
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
then

\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}u=f,\quad\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}\left(\left<\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>\right)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u)=0\text{ for all }\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}(T^{1,0}_{B})_{t_{o}}.

Proof.

First note that by Proposition
3.10

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u
is independent of the choice of representative
u
and the horizontal lift
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}
, and that by Proposition
4.5

\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}\left(\left<\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>\right)
is independent of the horizontal lift
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}
. It is also straightforward to see that in fact
\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}\left(\left<\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>\right)
does not depend on the choice of representative
u
of
f
. Indeed, in local coordinates on the base
B
as in Remark
3.2
, if
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are two representatives of
f
then
u_{1}-u_{2}=dp^{1}\wedge\gamma_{1}+...+dp^{m}\wedge\gamma_{m}
. Hence
\left<\bar{\partial}u_{1},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>-\left<\bar{\partial}u_{2},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>=dp^{1}\wedge\left<\bar{\partial}\gamma_{1},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>+...+dp^{m}\wedge\left<\bar{\partial}\gamma_{m},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>
, and the latter vanishes on the fibers of
p:X\to B
.

By
[
Vo-2007
, Proposition 9.5]
there exists an open set
U\subset B
containing
{t_{o}}
and diffeomorphism
F:=(\psi,p):p^{-1}(U)\to X_{t_{o}}\times U
that is tangent to the identity along
{t_{o}}
, such that for each
x\in X_{t_{o}}
the submanifold
U_{x}:=F^{-1}(\{x\}\times U)\subset p^{-1}(U)
is complex and
p|_{U_{x}}:U_{x}\to U
is biholomorphic. And though it’s not part of the statement in
[
Vo-2007
, Proposition 9.5]
,
F
is holomorphic at the fiber
X_{t_{o}}
, i.e., if
x\in X_{t_{o}}
and
h\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
then
\bar{\partial}(F^{*}h)((x,t_{o}))=0
. (Indeed, vanishing in the directions of
F^{-1}(\{x\}\times U)
follows from the construction, while in the vertical directions the map is the identity.) Let
\theta
be the integrable distribution on
p^{-1}(U)
obtained from that tangent bundles
T_{V_{x}}
,
x\in X_{t_{o}}
, via the diffeomorphism
F
. We set
\Phi:=F^{-1}
.

We show now that there is a smooth vector bundle isomorphism
\Psi:\Phi^{*}E\to p_{1}^{*}E_{t_{o}}
such that
d\Psi(x)\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}(x)=0
for all
x\in X_{t_{o}}
. The idea is similar to the proof of Ehresmann’s Theorem: one defines vector fields on the total space of
\Phi^{*}E
that project to constant vector fields on
U
. In order to get a vector bundle map, which is linear on fibers, one constructs vector fields that are linear on fibers. Fortunately the flows of vector fields that are linear on fibers cannot acquire enough vertical velocity to escape to infinity in finite time in the vertical direction. Thus lifts of constant vector fields (locally) on the base to vector fields on the vector bundle that are linear along fibers have flows by diffeomorphisms. The precise construction is as follows.

Fix a sufficiently small coordinate chart
U\subset B
and holomorphic coordinates
t
on
U
such that
t_{o}
is the origin. Choose a finite open cover
\{O_{1},...,O_{N}\}
of
X_{t_{o}}
by open sets on which
p_{1}^{*}E_{t_{o}}
is trivial, where
p_{1}:X_{t}\times U\to X_{t}
is the projection to the first factor, and let
U_{i}=O_{i}\times U
. Let
\{\chi_{1},...,\chi_{N}\}
be a partition of unity subordinate to
\{O_{1},...,O_{N}\}
. We will not need local coordinates on
X_{t_{o}}
, but we take coordinates on the fibers by fixing trivializing frames for
p_{1}^{*}E_{t_{o}}
over each
U_{j}
; if
U
is small enough then such frames exist. We write
v_{j}=(v_{j}^{1},...,v_{j}^{r})
for the components of a vector
v\in\Phi^{*}E
in terms of these frames. We let
t_{j}=(t_{j}^{1},...,t_{j}^{m})
be coordinates in the horizontal direction; they are the global coordinates
t
coming from the base
U
, but we keep the index because the corresponding constant vector fields
\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}^{\mu}}
on the trivial vector bundle over the chart
U_{j}
depend on
j
. The transition functions for these coordinates on the total space of
\Phi^{*}E
are

(x,t_{j},v_{j})=(x,t_{k},F_{jk}(x,t_{k})v_{k}),

where
F_{jk}(x,t_{k})
are invertible linear transformations that depend smoothly on
x
and
t_{k}
, and satisfy

\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\nu}_{k}}F_{jk}(x,t_{k})=0

for all
1\leq\nu\leq m
and
x\in O_{k}
. Now define the vector fields

\xi_{\nu}^{(j)}(x,t_{j},v_{j}):=\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}_{j}}.

Then on the fibers over
U_{j}\cap U_{k}
one has

\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}_{k}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}_{j}}+\frac{\partial(F_{jk}(x,t_{k})v_{k})^{\mu}}{\partial t_{j}^{\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{j}^{\mu}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\nu}_{j}}+\left(\frac{\partial F_{jk}(x,t_{k})}{\partial t_{j}^{\nu}}F_{kj}(x,t_{j})v_{j}\right)^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{j}^{\mu}}.

Therefore on fibers over
U_{j}\cap U_{k}
the difference of vector fields

\xi^{(k)}_{\nu}(x,t,F_{j,k}(x,t)v_{j})-\xi^{(j)}_{\nu}(x,t,v_{j})

is linear in
v_{j}
and holomorphic in
t
. It follows that the vector fields

\eta_{\nu}(v):=\sum_{j}\chi_{j}(x)\xi^{(j)}_{\nu}(x,t_{j},v_{j})

depend holomorphically on
t
and vary linearly in
v
in the fiber directions. By construction, these vector fields project onto the coordinate vector fields, and by integrating these vector fields we therefore get diffeomorphisms
\Psi^{s}_{\nu}\in{\rm Diff}((\Phi^{-1})^{*}E)
that are linear on fibers and that preserve the trivial family
X\times U\to U
. In fact, if
\phi:\Phi^{*}E\to X\times U
is the vector bundle projection then
\phi\Psi^{s}_{\nu}(x,t,v)=(x,t+s)
. Consider the diffeomorphism
\Psi:p_{1}^{*}E_{t}\to(\Phi^{-1})^{*}E
defined by

\Psi(v_{o},t):=\Psi_{1}^{t^{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\Psi_{m}^{t^{m}}(v_{o}).

Evidently

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\mu}}\Psi(v_{o},t)\right|_{t=0}=0

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\nu}}\Psi(v_{o},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}\Psi(v_{o},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\nu}}\left(\prod_{\lambda=1}^{\nu-1}d\Psi^{t^{\lambda}}(\Psi_{\lambda+1}^{t^{\lambda+1}}\circ\cdots\circ\Psi_{m}^{t^{m}}(v_{o}))\right)\eta_{\mu}(\Psi_{\mu}^{t^{\mu}}\circ\cdots\circ\Psi_{m}^{t^{m}}(v_{o})),

and since
\Psi_{j}^{0}={\rm Id}
we have

\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}^{\nu}}\Psi(v_{o},t)\right|_{t=0}=0.

Finally, to get the desired section
u
, we take the constant extension of
f
in the trivial family and pull it back to an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form via the diffeomorphisms
\Psi
and
F
. We need to check that
\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u)=0
and that
L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=0
. Writing
u_{o}(x,t):=f(x)
for the trivial extension of
f
, so that
u=F^{*}\Psi^{*}u_{o}
, our construction yields
\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}u=f
and, at the points of
X_{t_{o}}
,

\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}(F^{*}\Psi^{*}u_{o}))=F^{*}\Psi^{*}\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\tau}}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{o}\right)=0

and

L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}F^{*}\Psi^{*}\bar{\partial}u_{o}=F^{*}\Psi^{*}L^{1,0}_{\tau^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{\mu}}}\bar{\partial}u_{o}=0.

The proof is complete.
∎

We can now collect all we have done into the following simple but important theorem. (See Definitions
2.36
and
2.45
.i.)

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper holomorphic family of Hermitian holomorphic vector bundles and let
\theta\subset T_{X}
be any horizontal distribution. Define the substalk bundle
\Sigma\subset\tilde{\mathscr{H}}_{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}
by

\Sigma_{t}:=\left\{{\mathfrak{f}}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}})\ ;\ \iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}\text{ and }L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u=0\text{ for any }u\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E)_{t}\text{ representing }{\mathfrak{f}}\right\}.

Then
\Sigma
is independent of
\theta
, and formalizes
{\mathscr{H}}
. Moreover
({\mathscr{H}},\Sigma,{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})
is an integrable iBLS field.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.10
.a
\Sigma
is well-defined and by Proposition
3.10
.b
\Sigma
is independent of
\theta
. By Proposition
4.16
, every
f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
extends to a smooth section of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
that is holomorphic to second order at
t
. Therefore
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is bona fide, and by Proposition
3.10
.c
\Sigma
formalizes
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
. Moreover, by Proposition
4.16

\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}u=0
for any representative
u
of
f
, so
(\bar{\partial}^{\theta}{\mathfrak{f}})(t)=0\in\Lambda^{0,1}_{B,t}\otimes{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
. Finally, the fact that
{\mathscr{H}}
is integrable follows the proof of Proposition
4.16
; the reader can check that any representative
u
of the section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Sigma_{t_{o}}
satisfies
\left<\iota_{X_{t_{o}}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}u,\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}\wedge\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\right>=0
for all
\sigma,\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t_{o}}
.
∎

In fact,
({\mathscr{H}},\Sigma,{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})
is strongly iBLS. That is to say, the Gauss-Griffiths form

{\mathbb{G}}({\mathfrak{f}},{\mathfrak{g}}):=\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{\theta}/({\mathscr{H}},\Sigma)}{\mathfrak{f}},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathfrak{L}}^{\theta}/({\mathscr{H}},\Sigma)}{\mathfrak{g}}\right)

has the continuity property stated in Definition
2.45
.d. This fact is a consequence of the identity (
5
), whose proof is completed in Section
9
.

4.4.
Lift-independence of the relative curvature

For a choice of horizontal lift
\theta
the curvature of the BLS field
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is

\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}=\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}},

where
\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}
denotes the Chern connection of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
. One then has the relative curvature
\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})}({\mathscr{H}})
(see Definition
2.45
.iii) given by

\left(\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})}({\mathscr{H}})_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2}\right):=\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2}\right)-\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\sigma)f_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\tau)f_{2}\right),\quad t\in B,\ f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}.

The next result is key to a natural definition of the curvature of the iBLS field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of vector bundles. If
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are two horizontal lifts for
p
then

\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{1}})}({\mathscr{H}})=\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{2}})}({\mathscr{H}}).

The proof of Theorem
4.18
is given in Section
8
, after an explicit formula for the Chern connection of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is obtained.

Definition 4.19
.

The curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
with its
L^{2}
metric is

\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}:=\Theta^{({\rm rel}\ {\mathscr{L}}^{\theta})}({\mathscr{H}}),

where
\theta
is any horizontal lift for
p:X\to B
.

4.5.
Positivity of curvature for section fields

In this section we present some known but slightly less common multilinear algebra useful in the study of the curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
.

Quadratic forms on
2
-tensors

Positivity of the curvature
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
of a holomorphic vector bundle
{\mathfrak{H}}\to B
with metric
{\mathfrak{h}}
is a positivity property of the Hermitian form
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}
on the vector bundle
T^{1,0}_{B}\otimes{\mathfrak{H}}
defined in Paragraph
2.12
. One can generalize the latter notion of positivity to any Hermitian quadratic form
A
on a tensor product of vector spaces
M\otimes F
. (We use the letters
M
and
F
to indicate that the first and second factors correspond respectively to vectors in the manifold and fiber.) The elements of
M\otimes F
can be thought of as linear maps
M^{*}\to F
(or
F^{*}\to M
), and as such have a well-defined notion of rank. One can then write
M\otimes F
as an increasing union of subvarieties

\{0\}=:D_{o}(M\otimes F)\subset D_{1}(M\otimes F)\subset\cdots\subset D_{k}(M\otimes F)\subset...,

where
D_{k}(M\otimes F)
denotes the set of tensor of rank
\leq k
. Since the rank is at most
\min\{\dim M,\dim F)
, the subvarieties
D_{k}(M\otimes F)
stabilize. The sub-linearity of the rank also implies that

D_{k}(M\otimes F)+D_{k^{\prime}}(M\otimes F)\subset D_{k+k^{\prime}}(M\otimes F).

The subvarieties
D_{k}(M\otimes F)
are cones, and are non-linear if
k\neq 0
or
\min(\dim M,\dim F)
. Moreover, the generic element of
M\otimes F
has maximal rank, i.e.,
D_{\min(\dim M,\dim F)}(M\otimes F)
is Zariski open.

Definition 4.20
.

Let
M
and
F
be finite-dimensional vector spaces and suppose
F
is equipped with a Hermitian metric
\phi
.

a.

A Hermitian quadratic form
A
on
M\times F
is said to be
k
-positive if it is positive on
D_{k}(M\otimes F)
, i.e.,

D_{k}(M\otimes F)\ni\xi\mapsto A(\xi,\xi)\geq 0,

with equality if and only if
\xi=0
. (If the latter is omitted, we say
A
is
k
-nonnegative.)

b.

A tensor
Q\in{\rm Hom}(F,F)\otimes M\wedge\bar{M}
is said to be Hermitian for
\phi
if for all
\xi,\eta\in M
the endomorphism
Q_{\xi\bar{\eta}}:=\left<Q,\xi\otimes\bar{\eta}\right>
satisfies

\phi(Q_{\xi\bar{\eta}}v,w)=\phi(v,Q_{\xi\bar{\eta}}w).

c.

A Hermitian tensor
Q\in{\rm Hom}(F,F)\otimes M\wedge\bar{M}
is said to be
k
-positive if the Hermitian quadratic form

(M\otimes F)\otimes(\bar{M}\otimes\bar{F})\ni(\xi\otimes v,\bar{\eta}\otimes\bar{w})\mapsto\{(\xi\otimes v,\bar{\eta}\otimes\bar{w})\}_{Q,\phi}:=\phi(Q_{\xi\bar{\eta}}v,w)

is
k
-positive.

d.

A
1
-positive Hermitian form is also called Griffiths-positive, and a Hermitian form that is
k
-positive for every
k\geq 1
is also called Nakano-positive.

Thus the vector bundle
({\mathfrak{H}},{\mathfrak{h}})\to B
has
k
-positive curvature (Definition
2.49
) if and only if its curvature
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})
is
k
-positive if and only if
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{{\mathfrak{h}},\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})}
is
k
-positive.

These definitions can be extended to the infinite-rank case, but doing so requires some care. We shall not use these ideas in the infinite rank case.

Positivity for Schur complements

We wish to consider the situation in which the subspace
M
has a direct sum decomposition
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
. In this ‘split’ context we shall need a positivity statement that is best expressed in terms of the inertia map.

Recall that for a Hermitian vector space
W
with Hermitian metric
h
, one can associated to a Hermitian form
\Phi
its
inertia endomorphism

{\mathfrak{I}}(\Phi)={\mathfrak{I}}_{h}(\Phi)
defined by

h({\mathfrak{I}}(\Phi)v,w)=\Phi(v,w).

The
inertia map

{\mathfrak{I}}_{h}
is an isomorphism from the set of quadratic Hermitian forms on
W
onto the set of
h
-Hermitian endomorphisms of
W
.

We take the vector space
W
to be
M\otimes F
, where
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
. Suppose
F
has a Hermitian inner product
\phi
as before, and assume
M
is equipped with a Hermitian inner product
g
such that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
g
-orthogonal. (Write
g_{i}:=g|_{M_{i}}
.) We equip
M\otimes F
with the product metric
g\boxtimes\phi
. Then the inertia map
{\mathfrak{I}}={\mathfrak{I}}_{g\boxtimes\phi}
decomposes into four maps
{\mathfrak{I}}_{ij}
,
1\leq i,j\leq 2
, such that for any Hermitian quadratic form
\Phi
the maps

{\mathfrak{I}}_{ij}(\Phi):M_{i}\otimes F\to M_{j}\otimes F

satisfy
{\mathfrak{I}}_{ij}(\Phi)^{\dagger}={\mathfrak{I}}_{ji}(\Phi)
, where
\dagger
denotes Hermitian conjugation with respect to the relevant Hermitian metrics.

Definition 4.21
.

The Schur complement of
(\Phi,M_{1}\otimes F)
is the Hermitian form on
M_{1}\otimes F
whose inertia map with respect to the metric
g_{1}\boxtimes\phi
is

{\mathfrak{I}}_{11}(\Phi)-{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}(\Phi){\mathfrak{I}}_{22}(\Phi)^{-1}{\mathfrak{I}}_{21}(\Phi):M_{1}\otimes F\to M_{1}\otimes F.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\Phi
be an
m
-positive Hermitian form on
(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\otimes F
such that
\Phi|_{M_{2}\otimes F}
is Nakano-positive. Then the Schur complement of
(\Phi,M_{1}\otimes F)
is
m
-positive.

Proof.

Choose
f_{i},f_{j}\in F
, and define
f_{j}^{\dagger}\in F^{*}
by
f_{j}^{\dagger}f=\phi(f,f_{j})
for all
f\in F
. Also for
\xi\in M
define
\xi^{\ddagger}\in M^{*}
by
\xi^{\ddagger}\eta=g(\eta,\xi)
for all
\eta\in M
. For
\xi_{k}=(\tau_{k},w_{k}),\xi_{\ell}=(\tau_{\ell},w_{\ell})\in M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\xi_{k}\otimes f_{i},\xi_{\ell}\otimes f_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{11}f_{i}\right)\tau_{k}+w_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}f_{i}\right)\tau_{k}+\tau_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{21}f_{i}\right)w_{k}+w_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{22}f_{i}\right)w_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{11}f_{i}\right)\tau_{k}+w_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}f_{i}\right)\tau_{k}+\tau_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}^{\ddagger}f_{i}\right)w_{k}+w_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\left(f_{j}^{\dagger}{\mathfrak{I}}_{22}f_{i}\right)w_{k},

where the second equality holds because
{\mathfrak{I}}(\Phi)
is Hermitian. We can therefore define the
(1,1)
-form (a.k.a. sesquilinear form)

\Phi_{i\bar{j}}(\xi_{k},\bar{\xi}_{\ell}):=\Phi(\xi_{k}\otimes f_{i},\xi_{\ell}\otimes f_{j})

Now let us take
w_{k}:=-{\mathfrak{I}}_{22}(\Phi)^{-1}{\mathfrak{I}}_{21}(\Phi)\tau_{k}
,
1\leq k\leq m
. Then

\Phi_{i\bar{j}}(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell})=\tau_{\ell}^{\ddagger}\otimes f_{j}^{\dagger}\left({\mathfrak{I}}_{11}(\Phi)-{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}(\Phi){\mathfrak{I}}_{22}^{-1}(\Phi){\mathfrak{I}}_{21}(\Phi)\right)f_{i}\otimes\tau_{k}.

Setting
k=i
and
\ell=j
, and then summing, we obtain

\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\Phi_{i\bar{j}}(\xi_{i},\bar{\xi}_{j})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\tau_{j}^{\ddagger}\otimes f_{j}^{\dagger}\left({\mathfrak{I}}_{11}(\Phi)-{\mathfrak{I}}_{12}(\Phi){\mathfrak{I}}_{22}^{-1}(\Phi){\mathfrak{I}}_{21}(\Phi)\right)f_{i}\otimes\tau_{i},

and the result follows.
∎

4.6.
Semi-local descriptions of sections and representatives

For later computation it is useful to describe the actions of
\nabla^{1,0}
and
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
in terms of the description of smooth sections of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
, locally over the base, as
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on the total space
X
. We fix a local coordinate chart
U\subset B
and write
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
as in Remark
3.2
. Fix a representative
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
u
of a smooth section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
. Then
\nabla^{1,0}
is an
E
-valued
(n+1,0)
-form, so there exist global
(n,0)
-forms
\phi_{1},...,\phi_{m}
in
p^{-1}(U)
such that

(19)

\nabla^{1,0}u=dp^{1}\wedge\phi_{1}+\cdots+dp^{\mu}\wedge\phi_{\mu}.

The forms
\phi_{\mu}
depend on the representative
u
, but their restrictions to the fibers, i.e.,

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\phi_{\mu},\quad\mu=1,...,m,\ t\in U,

are uniquely determined by
{\mathfrak{f}}
, i.e., they are independent of the representative
u
of
{\mathfrak{f}}
.

For
\bar{\partial}
the situation is just slightly more subtle. One has

(20)

\bar{\partial}u=\psi+dp^{1}\wedge\alpha_{1}+\cdots+dp^{m}\wedge\alpha_{m}+d\bar{p}^{1}\wedge\beta_{\bar{1}}+\cdots+d\bar{p}^{m}\wedge\beta_{\bar{m}}

for some
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-forms
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m}
and
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms
\beta_{\bar{1}},...,\beta_{\bar{m}}
(that depend on the choice of local coordinates in
B
around
t
) and some
(n,1)
-form
\psi
. The forms
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m},\beta_{\bar{1}},...\beta_{\bar{m}}
and
\psi
are not unique, but for each
t\in U
the forms

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\alpha_{\mu}+\xi^{\theta}_{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}}\lrcorner\psi\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\beta_{\bar{\mu}}+\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}}\lrcorner\psi\right),\qquad\mu=1,...,m,

are uniquely determined.

The expression for
\bar{\partial}
gets slightly simpler if one is looking at sections of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
. (The case of sections of
{\mathscr{H}}
was explained in Remark
4.9
, but we collect it here again for convenience.)

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
E\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a holomorphic family of holomorphic vector bundles and let
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{L}})
. Let
u\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
be a representative of
{\mathfrak{f}}
. Fix a coordinate neighborhood
U\subset B
and write
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
in these coordinates.

a.

For some
t\in U
one has
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
if and only if
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\psi=0
in (
20
).

b.

The section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{L}}
restricts to a section of
{\mathscr{H}}
over
U
if and only if for any (and hence every) representative
u
one has (
20
) with
\psi\equiv 0
.

c.

The section
{\mathfrak{f}}
of
{\mathscr{L}}
is a holomorphic section of
{\mathscr{H}}|_{U}
if and only if for any (and hence every) representative
u
one has (
20
) with
\psi\equiv 0
,
\beta_{\bar{\nu}}\equiv 0
,
1\leq\nu\leq m
.

Proof.

Since
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\psi=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\bar{\partial}u=\bar{\partial}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}u
, (a) follows. From (a) we see that
{\mathfrak{f}}
restricts to a section of
{\mathscr{H}}
over
U
if and only if
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\psi=0
for every
t\in U
. We claim that the latter holds if and only if
\psi=dp^{\mu}\wedge\alpha^{\prime}_{\mu}+d\bar{p}^{\nu}\wedge\beta^{\prime}_{\bar{\nu}}
for some
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-forms
\alpha^{\prime}_{\mu}
and
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms
\beta^{\prime}_{\bar{\nu}}
,
1\leq\mu,\nu\leq m
. Indeed, this is clearly true locally (but with the global forms
dp^{\mu}
and
d\bar{p}^{\nu}
,
1\leq\mu,\nu\leq m
), and the local coefficient forms may be patched together using a partition of unity. This proves (b). Finally, suppose
{\mathfrak{f}}
is a section of
{\mathscr{H}}
over
U
. By (b) we can write
\bar{\partial}u=dp^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}+d\bar{p}^{\nu}\wedge\beta_{\bar{\nu}}
. Then
