{\mathfrak{f}}
is a holomorphic section if and only if for each
1\leq\mu\leq m
and every lift
\xi_{\mu}
of the
(1,0)
-vector field
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}
on
U
one has

0=\bar{\xi}_{\mu}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u=\beta_{\bar{\mu}}-d\bar{p}^{\nu}\wedge(\bar{\xi}_{\mu}\lrcorner\beta_{\bar{\nu}}).

Thus
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\beta_{\bar{\mu}}\equiv 0
for all
t\in U
. But for an
(n,0)
-form we already know that this condition is equivalent to the existence of a decomposition
\beta_{\bar{\mu}}=dt^{\nu}\wedge\gamma_{\nu\bar{\mu}}
, which proves (c).
∎

We emphasize that the forms
\phi_{\mu}
associated to
\nabla^{1,0}u
and
\alpha_{\mu}
,
\beta_{\bar{\mu}}
, and
\psi
associated to
\bar{\partial}u
are
global
on the preimage
p^{-1}(U)
of the coordinate chart
U
.

5.
Local Triviality

5.1.
L^{2}
Extension

The question of local triviality of vector bundles like
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
has been of interest since the beginning of the theory of deformations of complex manifolds introduced by Kodaira and Spencer. The traditional approach is based on the observation that if the dimensions of the fibers are constant then the fibration is locally trivial.

While this perspective is broad in its scope, its application can be very difficult in most cases. But a new approach has become very popular in the past two decades or so. It is well-known that the dimensions of the fibers of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
vary upper semi-continuously with the point of the base
B
. Thus to prove local triviality, one need only prove lower semi-continuity of these dimensions. The latter is a consequence of the existence of linear extension operators. A powerful tool that has many other applications is the Ohsawa-Takegoshi Theory of
L^{2}
-Extension. This theory consists of a huge and growing collection of extension theorems obtained by
L^{2}
methods of one kind or another. For applications in the present article the following result can be used.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
Y
be a weakly pseudoconvex Kähler manifold of complex dimension
N
, let
Z\subset Y
be a closed complex submanifold of codimension
1
, and let
E\to Y
be a holomorphic vector bundle. Assume there exist smooth Hermitian metrics
h
for
E
and
e^{-\lambda}
for the line bundle
L_{Z}\to Y
associated to
Z
, a holomorphic section
T\in H^{0}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}(L_{Z}))
whose zero divisor is
Z
counting multiplicity, and a number
\delta>0
such that

\sup_{Y}|T|^{2}e^{\lambda}\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h)\geq\delta\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\partial\bar{\partial}\lambda\otimes{\rm Id}_{E}\geq 0\text{ in the sense of Nakano}.

Then for every holomorphic section
f\in H^{0}(Z,{\mathcal{O}}(K_{Z}\otimes E|_{Z}))
such that

\int_{Z}\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{(N-1)^{2}}\left<f\wedge\bar{f},h\right><+\infty

there exists
F\in H^{0}(Y,{\mathcal{O}}(K_{Y}\otimes E\otimes L_{Z}))
such that

F|_{Z}=f\wedge dT\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{Y}\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{N^{2}}\left<F\wedge\bar{F},h\otimes e^{-\lambda}\right>\leq C_{\delta}\int_{Z}\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{(N-1)^{2}}\left<f\wedge\bar{f},h\right>.

If the manifold
Y
in Theorem
5.1
is compact Kähler then perhaps the shortest proof of Theorem
5.1
is via Berndtsson’s method of currents; see the appendix of
[
B-2009
]
in the case in which
E
has rank
1
. A proof in the higher rank case can be found in
[
R-2014
]
.

5.2.
Sufficient conditions for local triviality

We start with Berndtsson’s local triviality result mentioned in the introduction.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles. If the metric
h
has non-negative curvature in the sense of Nakano then
{\mathscr{H}}
is a holomorphic vector bundle.

Proof.

Any of the
L^{2}
extension theorems mentioned in the previous paragraph imply that the dimensions of the fibers
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
vary lower semicontinuously with
t
. Upper semicontinuity, which holds for all smooth metrics
h
regardless of their curvature, is a consequence of Montel’s Theorem.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
be a proper Kähler family of holomorphic Hermitian vector bundles. Assume that
\Theta({\mathfrak{h}})\geq-Cp^{*}\omega\otimes{\rm Id}_{E}
for some positive Hermitian form
\omega
on
B
and some positive continuous function
C
on
X
. Then
{\mathscr{H}}
is a holomorphic vector bundle.

Proof.

We may assume that the base
B
is the unit ball and that the metric
h
is defined up to the boundary
p^{-1}(\partial B)
of
X
. Then for
M>>0
the metric
h_{M}:=e^{-M|t|^{2}}h
for
E\to X
has Nakano-nonnegative curvature, and hence by Proposition
5.2
the associated Hilbert field
{\mathscr{H}}_{M}\to B
is locally trivial. But the underlying vector bundles for
{\mathscr{H}}_{M}
and
{\mathscr{H}}
are the same.
∎

5.3.
Examples of Non-locally trivial
{\mathscr{H}}

There are many examples of smooth proper holomorphic families
E\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
for which
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is not locally trivial.

Trivial family of manifolds

The simplest examples come from trivial holomorphic families
p:X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
with
X_{o}
a compact Riemann surface of genus
g\geq 1
together with a non-trivial family of holomorphic line bundles
E\to X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}
, where
{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the unit disk. The examples are based on the following simple

Fact:

There is no meromorphic function on
X_{o}
with exactly one pole, whose order is
1
.

Indeed, since the number of preimages of any point under a nonconstant holomorphic map of compact Riemann surfaces is independent of the point, the existence of such a meromorphic function would yield an isomorphism
X_{o}\cong{\mathbb{P}}_{1}
, which is impossible.

It follows that the line bundle
L_{D}
associated to the divisor
D=p-q
is either trivial, i.e.,
p=q
, in which case
H^{0}(X_{o},{\mathcal{O}}(L_{D}))={\mathbb{C}}
, or else
p\neq q
, in which case
H^{0}(X_{o},{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{o}}(L_{D}))=\{0\}
. Indeed, suppose
p\neq q
and
s\in H^{0}(X_{o},{\mathcal{O}}(L_{D}))
. Let
s_{D}
be a meromorphic section with divisor
D
. If
s
does not vanish at
p
then the function
s/s_{D}
is meromorphic with exactly one pole, which is impossible. Therefore
s
vanishes at
p
, in which case
s/s_{D}
is holomorphic, hence constant. But
s/s_{D}
vanishes at
q
, so
s/s_{D}\equiv 0
. Hence
s=0
as claimed.

Now let
f:{\mathbb{D}}\to X_{o}
be an injective holomorphic map with
f(0)=p
and consider the holomorphic family of line bundles
E_{f}\to X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}
defined by

E_{f}:=K^{*}_{X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes L_{D_{f}},

where
D_{f}
is the divisor on
X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}
defined by

D_{f}:=\{p\}\times{\mathbb{D}}-\{(f(t),t)\ ;\ t\in{\mathbb{D}}\}

and
p_{1}:X_{o}\times{\mathbb{D}}\to X_{o}
is the Cartesian projection. Then

{\mathscr{H}}_{t}=H^{0}(X_{o},{\mathcal{O}}(L_{p-f(t)}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{,\ }l}{\mathbb{C}}&t=0\\
\{0\}&t\in{\mathbb{D}}-\{0\}\end{array}\right..

Thus
{\mathscr{H}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
is not locally trivial.

Nontrivial families of manifolds

Another very simple example is constructed as follows. Let
\mathbf{H}\subset{\mathbb{C}}
denote the (open) upper half plane and consider the family of tori

X:=({\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbf{H})/\sim,

where
(z,t)\sim(\zeta,\tau)\iff t=\tau
and
\zeta-z=m+nt
for some
m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. Of course
p:X\to\mathbf{H}
is the map induced by the projection
{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
. The fibers
X_{t}
are tori, which we identify with the quotient
{\mathbb{C}}/({\mathbb{Z}}+t{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Let
D_{a}
denote the smooth hypersurface on
X
obtained from
\{a\}\times\mathbf{H}\subset{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbf{H}
after passing to the quotient. We take
E
to be the line bundle associated to the divisor
D:=D_{0}-D_{\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}}
. The two smooth hypersurfaces
D_{0}
and
D_{\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}}
intersect at the points
[(0,t)]\in X
whenever
m+nt=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}
for some
m,n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(such
t
form a closed discrete subset of
\mathbf{H}
), but are otherwise are disjoint. Denote by
B\subset\mathbf{H}
the set of such
t
. Then the divisors
\Delta_{t}:=D\cap X_{t}
are trivial if
t\in B
, and otherwise are
[0]-[t]
.

Since the canonical bundle of any torus is trivial, we see that

{\mathscr{H}}_{t}=H^{0}(X_{t},{\mathcal{O}}(E_{t}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{,\ }l}{\mathbb{C}}&t\in B\\
\{0\}&t\in\mathbf{H}\setminus B\end{array}\right..

6.
Elements of Hodge Theory

The twisted Laplace Beltrami operator for a Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle
(E,h)
on a Hermitian manifold
(X,g)
is

\Box:=\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}^{*}+\bar{\partial}^{*}\bar{\partial};

while the
\bar{\partial}
-operator depends only on
X
and
E
, and not on the metrics, the formal adjoint
\bar{\partial}^{*}
does carry information about the metrics
g
and
h
. In particular, the Kernel

{\rm Harm}(g,h)=\bigoplus_{0\leq p,q\leq n}{\rm Harm}^{p,q}(g,h)

depends on these metrics.

6.1.
The Hodge Theorem

The Hodge Theorem can be stated as follows.

Theorem 6.1

(Hodge Theorem for Vector Bundle-valued Forms)
.

Let
(X,g)
be a compact complex Hermitian manifold and let
(E,h)\to X
be a holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle. Then the vector spaces
{\rm Harm}^{p,q}(g,h)
are finite dimensional and consist of smooth
E
-valued
(p,q)
-forms. Moreover, with

\wp_{p,q}:L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)\to{\rm Harm}^{p,q}(g,h)

denoting the orthogonal projection, there exists a compact self-adjoint collection of operators

G_{p,q}:L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)\to L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h),\quad 0\leq p,q\leq n,

called the Green operator, such that

(a)

G_{p,q}W^{s,2}_{p,q}(X)\subset W^{s+2,2}_{p,q}(X)
for all
s\geq 0
,

(b)

G_{p,q}{\rm Harm}^{p,q}(g,h)=0
,
[G,\bar{\partial}]=[G,\bar{\partial}^{*}]=0
and
||G_{p,q}||\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{1}_{p,q}}
, where
\lambda^{1}_{p,q}
is the smallest positive eigenvalue of
\Box
in
L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)
, and

(c)

one has the orthogonal decomposition
{\rm Id}=\wp_{p,q}+\Box G_{p,q}
on
L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)
.

From the Chern connection
\nabla=\nabla^{1,0}+\bar{\partial}
one defines the
\nabla^{1,0}
-Laplacian

\Box^{1,0}:=\nabla^{1,0}\nabla^{1,0*}+\nabla^{1,0*}\nabla^{1,0}

and its associated Green operator
G^{1,0}
(whose action on
(p,q)
-forms will be unfortunately denoted
G^{1,0}_{p,q}
; we shall eliminate the subscript whenever there is no confusion). An analogous Hodge Theorem holds for
\Box^{1,0}
.

6.2.
Kähler manifolds

Suppose
X
is a Kähler manifold and
\omega
is a Kähler form on
X
. Let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle. An
E
-valued
(p,q)
-form may also be seen as a section of the vector bundle
\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}\otimes E\to X
. The latter vector bundle is not holomorphic unless
q=0
, but there is a smooth isomorphism

\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}\otimes E\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{\omega}}}{{\to}}\Lambda^{p,0}_{X}\otimes\Lambda^{q}(T^{1,0}_{X})\otimes E

obtained from the identification of
(1,0)
-vectors and
(0,1)
-forms via the Kähler metric, given by
T^{1,0}_{X}\ni v\mapsto v\lrcorner\omega\in T^{*0,1}_{X}
. Then one defines

\bar{\mathfrak{d}}:=\Phi_{\omega}^{-1}\bar{\partial}\Phi_{\omega}:\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}\otimes E))\to\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{0,1}_{X}\otimes\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}\otimes E))

and with the induced
L^{2}
inner product one defines the formal adjoint
\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{*}
, and consequently the Bochner-Kodaira Laplacian
\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{*}\bar{\mathfrak{d}}
(also called the
rough Laplacian
in differential geometry circles). So far nothing about the Kähler nature of
X
has been used, but the next identity relies heavily on the Kähler assumption.

Theorem 6.2

(Bochner-Kodaira Identity)
.

Let
X
be a Kähler manifold with Kähler metric
g
and let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with Hermitian metric
h
. Then one has the identity

\Box=\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{*}\bar{\mathfrak{d}}+\Theta(h\otimes g^{(p)}\otimes\det g)^{\sharp}\lrcorner,

where
g^{(p)}
and
\det(g)
are the metrics induced on
\Lambda^{p,0}_{X}
and
K_{X}^{*}
respectively, and for a vector-bundle-valued
(1,1)
-form
A\in\Gamma(X,\Lambda^{1,1}_{X}\otimes{\rm Hom}(F,F))
we denote by
A^{\sharp}
the
T^{1,0}_{X}\otimes{\rm Hom}(F,F)
-valued
(1,0)
-form obtained by raising with respect to the Kähler metric.

One can also carry out the same procedure for the
(1,0)
-Laplacian

\Box^{1,0}:=\nabla^{1,0}\nabla^{1,0*}+\nabla^{1,0*}\nabla^{1,0},

and define the operator
{\mathfrak{d}}=\Phi_{\omega}^{-1}\nabla^{1,0}\Phi_{\omega}
. The corresponding identity is

\Box^{1,0}={\mathfrak{d}}^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}+\Theta(h\otimes g^{(p)}\otimes\det g)^{\bar{\sharp}}\lrcorner,

where
\bar{\sharp}
corresponds to the raising of a
(1,0)
-form to a
(0,1)
-vector field. One can then show
[
Si-1982
]
that

(21)

\Box-\Box^{1,0}=[\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h),\Lambda_{\omega}],

where
\Lambda_{\omega}
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of the so-called
Lefschetz operator

L_{\omega}:\alpha\mapsto\omega\wedge\alpha.

Alternatively, one can arrive at (
21
) by using the (twisted)
Hodge identities

[\bar{\partial}^{*},L_{\omega}]=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\nabla^{1,0}\quad\text{and}\quad[\nabla^{1,0*},L_{\omega}]=-\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\bar{\partial},

and their adjoints

[\Lambda_{\omega},\bar{\partial}]=-\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\nabla^{1,0*}\quad\text{and}\quad[\Lambda_{\omega},\nabla^{1,0}]=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\bar{\partial}^{*}.

Remark
.

If
\Theta(h)=0
or
(p,q)=(n,0)
then by (
21
) one has
\Box=\Box^{1,0}
and
G_{p,q}=G^{1,0}_{p,q}
.

\diamond

6.3.
Pointwise Lefschetz Theory

In the proofs of Corollary
1.2
and Theorem
3
primitive twisted forms play a central role. In the present paragraph we recall some basic facts about these forms, and the general Lefschetz Theory.

Classical, untwisted case

The Lefschetz operator has a central role in the study of Kähler manifolds. We summarize a few of its salient features here, but the reader will find a more complete discussion in many texts; a few excellent sources are
[
GH-1978
]
,
[
H-2005
]
and
[
Vo-2007
]
.

The starting point is the commutation relation

(22)

[L_{\omega},\Lambda_{\omega}]u=(p+q-n)u,\quad\text{u}\in\Gamma(X,\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}).

Thus
[[L_{\omega},\Lambda_{\omega}],L_{\omega}]=2L_{\omega}
and
[[L_{\omega},\Lambda_{\omega}],\Lambda_{\omega}]=-2\Lambda_{\omega}
. Consequently for any point
x\in X
the operators
X:=L_{\omega}
,
Y:=\Lambda_{\omega}
and
H:=[L_{\omega},\Lambda_{\omega}]
form a representation of
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
on
\Lambda^{p,q}_{X,x}
. The theory of finite-dimensional representations of
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}({\mathbb{C}})
is relatively simple, and a central result is that in every finite-dimensional irreducible representation the Kernel of
Y
is 1-dimensional and the representation is spanned by the images of this kernel under
X^{k}
,
k=0,1,...
. A non-zero vector in the kernel of
Y
is called a
lowest weight vector
, or a
primitive
vector. One shows that if
u\in\Gamma(X,\Lambda^{p,q}_{X})
and
\Lambda_{\omega}u=0
then
L_{\omega}^{n+1-(p+q)}u=0
. A study of the orbits of
L_{\omega}
in
\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}
yields the so-called Lefschetz decomposition of forms:

Proposition 6.3

(Lefschetz Decomposition)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
k\in\{0,...,2n\}
. For every
k
-form
\alpha
on
X
there exist unique primitive
(k-2j)
-forms
\alpha_{j}
,
\max(0,n-k)\leq j\leq k
, such that

\alpha=\sum_{j}L_{\omega}^{j}\alpha_{j}.

Moreover, the summands are pointwise pairwise orthogonal.

The metric induced on
(k,0)
-forms by the Kähler form has a particularly simple form: one has the identity

\left<\alpha,\beta\right>\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{k^{2}}\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}.

This identity does not persist for
k
forms,
k\leq n
, of more general bi-degree, but remarkably it
almost
holds for primitive forms: the right hand side is either equal to the left hand side or to its negative. The result is known as the Hodge-Riemann Bilinear Relations.

Theorem 6.4

(Hodge-Riemann Bilinear Relations)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a Kähler manifold of complex dimension
n
. If
\alpha
is a
(p,q)
-form with
p+q\leq n
and
\Lambda_{\omega}\alpha=0
then

\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{(p+q)^{2}}\alpha\wedge\bar{\alpha}\wedge\frac{\omega^{n-(p+q)}}{(n-(p+q))!}=(-1)^{q}\left<\alpha,\alpha\right>\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}.

Remark
.

Of greatest interest to us is the case
(p,q)=(n-1,1)
, in which the pairing

\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\alpha\wedge\bar{\beta}=-\left<\alpha,\bar{\beta}\right>\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}

is negative definite.

\diamond

The untwisted Hodge Identity
[\bar{\partial}^{*},L_{\omega}]=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\partial
shows that

L_{\omega}\Box=\bar{\partial}(\bar{\partial}^{*}L_{\omega}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\partial)+(\bar{\partial}^{*}L_{\omega}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\partial)\bar{\partial}=\Box L_{\omega}+\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}(\bar{\partial}\partial+\partial\bar{\partial})=\Box L_{\omega}.

Thus on a Kähler manifold the Lefschetz operator induces a well-defined map on Dolbeault cohomology. By focusing on the primitive harmonic forms one can extend the pointwise Lefschetz theory just discussed to the setting of cohomology via the Fundamental Theorem of Hodge Theory: one says that a Dolbeault class
\phi
is
primitive
if its unique harmonic representative is a primitive form.

Though it seems to be less commonly done in the algebraic geometry literature, one may also describe primitive Dolbeault cohomology classes in more Dolbeault-cohomological terms, i.e., without reference to harmonic forms. In fact, since
\omega
commutes with
\bar{\partial}
, a Dolbeault
(p,q)
-class
\phi
is primitive if and only if for any
\alpha\in\phi
the form
\omega^{n+1-(p+q)}\wedge\alpha
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact. It is this equivalent way of defining primitive cohomology that allows the definition to extend to the twisted setting. Indeed, in the untwisted setting a primitive harmonic form always defines a primitive cohomology class: if
k\leq n
and
\alpha
is a primitive harmonic
k
-form then
\omega^{n+1-k}\wedge(\alpha+\bar{\partial}\beta)=\bar{\partial}(\omega^{n-k+1}\wedge\beta)
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact. However, in the twisted setting the harmonic representative of a primitive Dolbeault class is not necessarily primitive. (See Remark
6.6
.)

Twisted case

Now consider the twisted setting of forms with values in a holomorphic Hermitian vector bundle
E\to X
with Hermitian metric
h
. Because twisted forms are sections of
\Lambda_{X}^{*}\otimes E\to X
and the Lefschetz operator acts only on the first factor, one can define primitive forms in exactly the same way as in the flat, or untwisted, case.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a Kähler manifold of complex dimension
n
, let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle, and fix integers
p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
p+q\leq n
.

(1)

A Dolbeault class
C\in H^{p,q}_{\bar{\partial}}(X,E)
is
primitive
if for every
\alpha\in C
the
E
-valued
(n+1-q,n+1-p)
-form
\alpha\wedge\omega^{n+1-(p+q)}
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact.

(2)

An
E
-valued form
\alpha
of degree
k\leq n
is
primitive
if
\alpha\wedge\omega^{n+1-k}=0
, or equivalently if
\Lambda_{g}\alpha=0
.

Remark 6.6
.

In the untwisted case, if
C
is a primitive Dolbeault class then its harmonic representative is necessarily primitive. Indeed, if
\alpha_{o}\in C
is harmonic then
\alpha_{o}\wedge\omega^{n+1-k}
is
\bar{\partial}
-exact and harmonic, and must therefore vanish. Thus an untwisted Dolbeault class is primitive if and only if it contains a primitive form.

In the twisted setting the harmonic representative of a primitive Dolbeault class
C
need no longer be primitive. The argument above breaks down because

L_{\omega}\Box=\Box L_{\omega}+\Theta(h),

so the Lefschetz operator no longer preserves harmonicity. Nevertheless there does exist at least one primitive form in
C
. The argument is not difficult, but for maximum simplicity (and since it is the only case we use) we present it in the case where
C
is of bidegree
(n-1,1)
. If
u_{o}\in C
then one has
\omega\wedge u_{o}=\bar{\partial}\phi
. Since
\phi
has bidegree
(n,1)
, there exists a twisted
(n-1,0)
-form
\psi
such that
\omega\wedge\psi=\phi
. Indeed, one can take
\psi=-\Lambda_{\omega}\phi
and use (
22
). Now simply take
u=u_{o}-\bar{\partial}\psi
. Then
\omega\wedge u=\bar{\partial}\phi-\omega\wedge\bar{\partial}\psi=0
because
L_{\omega}
commutes with
\bar{\partial}
.

For forms of degree
k<n
the argument is similar, but in order to solve the equation
\omega^{n+1-k}\wedge u_{o}=\bar{\partial}\phi
one must use the commutation relation (
22
) in a more clever way.

\diamond

The Lefschetz Decomposition Theorem
6.3
immediately extends to twisted forms.

Proposition 6.7

(Twisted Lefschetz Decomposition)
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a Kähler manifold and let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle. Let
k\in\{0,...,2n\}
. For every
E
-valued
k
-form
\alpha
on
X
there exist unique primitive
E
-valued
(k-2j)
-forms
\alpha_{j}
,
\max(0,n-k)\leq j\leq k
, such that

\alpha=\sum_{j}\omega^{j}\wedge\alpha_{j}.

Moreover, the summands are pointwise pairwise orthogonal.

There is also a version of the pointwise Hodge-Riemann Bilinear Relations.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
E\to X,h
be a Hermitian holomorphic vector bundle on a compact Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
. If an
E
-valued
(p,q)
-form
\alpha
is primitive then

\frac{\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{(p+q)^{2}}}{(n-(p+q))!}\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\alpha}\wedge\omega^{n-(p+q)},h\right>=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{p-q}|\alpha|^{2}_{g,h}dV_{\omega}.

Partial proof.

We prove the result in the simplest special case
(p,q)=(n-1,1)
. The simplest proof we know uses Kähler coordinates vanishing at a point
x\in X
: in these coordinates

\omega(x)=\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\partial\bar{\partial}|z|^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha(x)=\alpha_{i\bar{j}}(x)dz^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{dz^{i}}\wedge...\wedge dz^{n}\wedge d\bar{z}^{j}

for some
\alpha_{i\bar{j}}(x)\in E_{x}
,
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Then
\alpha
is primitive if and only if
\alpha_{i\bar{j}}(x)=0
for all
1\leq i\neq j\leq n
. Thus

\displaystyle\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\alpha},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(\alpha_{i\bar{i}}(x),\alpha_{j\bar{j}}(x))dz^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{dz^{i}}\wedge...\wedge dz^{n}\wedge d\bar{z}^{i}\wedge d\bar{z}^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{d\bar{z}^{j}}\wedge...\wedge d\bar{z}^{n}\wedge dz^{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta^{i\bar{j}}h(\alpha_{i\bar{i}}(x),\alpha_{j\bar{j}}(x))\frac{dV(z)}{\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n}(-1)^{n(n+1)/2}}=-\delta^{i\bar{j}}h(\alpha_{i\bar{i}}(x),\alpha_{j\bar{j}}(x))\frac{dV(z)}{\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}},

i.e.,
\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\left<\alpha\wedge\bar{\alpha},h\right>=-|\alpha|^{2}_{g,h}dV_{\omega}
, as claimed.
∎

Remark
.

The general case is proved using Weil’s Formula for the Hodge star operator. See
[
Vo-2007
, Proposition 6.29]
.

\diamond

Remark
.

In the twisted setting the pointwise Hodge-Riemann pairing
Q(\alpha,\beta)
is an
E\otimes E
-valued
(n,n)
-form, so the integrated form of the Hodge-Riemann pairing
{\mathcal{Q}}
no longer makes sense. To the best of our knowledge there is no obvious twisted analogue of the Hodge Index Theorem.

\diamond

6.4.
Solutions of the
\bar{\partial}
Equation with minimal
L^{2}
-norm

The Hodge Theorem yields solutions of the
\bar{\partial}
-equation with estimates. The statement is the following.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
X
be a compact complex manifold equipped with a smooth Hermitian metric
g
, let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle equipped with a smooth Hermitian metric
h
, and let
q\geq 1
. For every
\bar{\partial}
-closed
E
-valued
(p,q)
-form
\alpha
such that
\wp_{p,q}\alpha=0
there exists a unique
E
-valued
(p,q-1)
-form
u_{o}
such that

(23)

\bar{\partial}u_{o}=\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad||u_{o}||^{2}=\sup_{\gamma\in\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{p,q}_{X}\otimes E))}\frac{\left|\left(\alpha,\gamma\right)\right|^{2}}{||\bar{\partial}\gamma||^{2}+||\bar{\partial}^{*}\gamma||^{2}}.

The
L^{2}
-norm of any other solution
u
of the equation
\bar{\partial}u=\alpha
is larger.

Proof.

Let
H_{o}
be the collection of smooth
E
-valued
(p,q)
-forms that are orthogonal to
{\rm Kernel}(\Box)
and let
H
be the Hilbert space closure of
H_{o}
with respect to the norm

||\gamma||^{2}_{H}:=||\bar{\partial}\gamma||^{2}+||\bar{\partial}^{*}\gamma||^{2}.

The latter is a norm because by the Hodge Theorem there exists a constant
c>0
such that
(\Box\gamma,\gamma)\geq c||\gamma||^{2}
for all
\gamma\in H
. Let
\lambda(\gamma):=(\alpha,\gamma)
. Then
\lambda\in H^{*}
and

||\lambda||^{2}_{H^{*}}=C:=\sup_{\gamma\in H_{o}}\frac{\left|\left(\alpha,\gamma\right)\right|^{2}}{||\bar{\partial}\gamma||^{2}+||\bar{\partial}^{*}\gamma||^{2}}.

By the Riesz Representation Theorem there exists
\beta\in H
such that
||\beta||_{H}=||\lambda||_{H^{*}}
and
\lambda(\gamma)=(\beta,\gamma)_{H}
. The latter means precisely that
\Box\beta=\alpha
(in the weak sense). Since
\bar{\partial}\alpha=0
,

0=(\bar{\partial}\Box\beta,\bar{\partial}\beta)=(\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}^{*}\bar{\partial}\beta,\bar{\partial}\beta)=||\bar{\partial}^{*}\bar{\partial}\beta||^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad 0=(\bar{\partial}^{*}\bar{\partial}\beta,\beta)=||\bar{\partial}\beta||^{2},

so
\Box\beta=\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}^{*}\beta
. Therefore
u_{o}:=\bar{\partial}^{*}\beta
solves the equation
\bar{\partial}u_{o}=\alpha
, and
||u_{o}||^{2}=||\beta||^{2}_{H}=C
. Finally, since
u_{o}=\bar{\partial}^{*}\beta
,
u_{o}
is orthogonal to the kernel of
\bar{\partial}
, and hence is the unique solution of minimal norm. The proof is complete.
∎

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
X
,
E
,
g
,
h
and
\alpha
be as in Proposition
6.9
.

i.

There exists a solution
u
of the equation
\bar{\partial}u=\alpha
such that

||u||^{2}\leq(\lambda^{1}_{p,q})^{-1}||\alpha||^{2},

where
\lambda^{1}_{p,q}
is the smallest positive eigenvalue of
\Box:L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)\to L^{2}_{p,q}(g,h)
.

ii.

Moreover, if the metric
g
is Kähler and the endomorphism

A:=\Theta(h\otimes g^{(p)}\otimes\det(g))^{\sharp}\lrcorner

(c.f. Theorem
6.2
)
is positive semi-definite,
\alpha\perp{\rm Kernel}(A)
almost everywhere on the support of
\alpha
, and
(A^{-1}\alpha,\alpha)<+\infty
then there exists an
E
-valued
(p,q-1)
-form
u
satisfying the Demailly-Skoda-Hörmander Estimate

||u||^{2}\leq(A^{-1}\alpha,\alpha)

such that
\bar{\partial}u=\alpha
. Consequently

(G\alpha,\alpha)\leq(A^{-1}\alpha,\alpha).

Proof.

By Proposition
6.9
the Hodge Theorem and the minimal solution is
u_{o}=G\bar{\partial}^{*}\alpha
. Then

||u_{o}||^{2}=(G\bar{\partial}^{*}\alpha,G\bar{\partial}^{*}\alpha)=(G\alpha,\bar{\partial}\bar{\partial}^{*}G\alpha)=(G\alpha,\alpha).

Thus i follows because
G=(\Box|_{{\rm Harm}^{\perp}})^{-1}
, and ii follows from the existence of a solution
u
with the estimate
||u||^{2}\leq(A^{-1}\alpha,\alpha)
; a consequence of Skoda’s and Demailly’s modifications of Hörmander’s
L^{2}
estimate.
∎

6.5.
The Bergman projection and the Neumann operator

For a general family
(E,h)\to X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}B
, i.e., not necessarily proper, with
h
smooth (also not necessary, but some regularity may be required) the Hilbert spaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
are closed Hilbert subspaces of
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
. (In our proper setting of a proper holomorphic family the spaces
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}={\rm Harm}_{n,0}(g_{t},h_{t})
are finite dimensional, so closedness is automatic.) The corresponding bounded orthogonal projection

P_{t}:{\mathscr{L}}_{t}\to{\mathscr{H}}_{t}

is called the
Bergman projection
.

From a section
f\in\Gamma(X,K_{X/B}\otimes E)
that is smooth (or even just
L^{2}
) on the fibers of
p
one has a section
{\mathcal{P}}f\in\Gamma(X,K_{X/B}\otimes E)
defined by

\iota_{t}{\mathcal{P}}f:=P_{t}\iota_{t}f

where
\iota_{t}:\Gamma(X_{t},(K_{X/B}\otimes E)|_{X_{t}})\to\Gamma(X_{t},K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}})
is the isomorphism defined by Proposition
3.1
. Thus
P_{{\mathscr{H}}}:{\mathscr{L}}\to{\mathscr{H}}
is given by

P_{{\mathscr{H}}}{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left({\mathcal{P}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right).

The orthogonal projection
P_{t}^{\perp}:{\mathscr{L}}_{t}\to{\mathscr{H}}_{t}^{\perp}
onto the orthogonal complement of
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
in
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
is called the
Neumann operator
. If one is given a
K_{X_{t}}\otimes E|_{X_{t}}
-valued,
\bar{\partial}
-exact
(0,1)
-form
\alpha
then any two solutions
u_{o},u_{1}\in{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
of the equation
\bar{\partial}u=\alpha
differ by an element of
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
. Thus if
u\in{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
satisfies
\bar{\partial}u=\alpha
then so does
P_{t}^{\perp}u
, and the latter has minimal
{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
-norm.

On a compact complex manifold the Hodge Theorem computes
P_{t}
and
P_{t}^{\perp}
. Indeed, by the Hodge Theorem every
\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}
-exact form is orthogonal to the harmonic forms, and, as we saw in the proof of Theorem
, if
\alpha=\Box_{X_{t}}u
is
\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}
-closed then
\alpha=\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}u
. Thus
\wp(\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f)=0
(i.e.,
\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f
has no harmonic part) and
\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f=\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f
. Since
f-\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f
is holomorphic and
(\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f,f_{o})=(G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f,\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f_{o})=0
for any
f_{o}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
, we see that

(24)

P_{t}f=f-\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f\quad\text{ and thus }\quad P_{t}^{\perp}f=\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}^{*}G_{t}\bar{\partial}_{X_{t}}f.

Remark 6.11
.

It is natural to ask whether
{\mathcal{P}}
(and hence
{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}={\rm Id}-{\mathcal{P}}
) is a smooth operator. In view of (
24
) it suffices to decide when the Green operator
G_{t}
varies smoothly with
t
. The latter question was answered by Kodaira and Spencer: if the rank of the harmonic spaces is constant then
G_{t}
varies smoothly with
t
(and hence
{\mathcal{P}}^{\perp}
and
{\mathcal{P}}
are smooth operators). See
[
K-1986
, Theorem 7.6, page 344]
. And though it is not stated explicitly in the latter reference, the converse is also true: if the Green operator
G_{t}
varies smoothly with
t
then the dimensions of the harmonic spaces are locally constant.

Thus, in the sense of Definition
2.23
.c,
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}
is regular if and only if
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is a vector bundle.

\diamond

7.
The Kodaira-Spencer Map of a Proper Holomorphic Family

The Kodaira-Spencer map of a germ at
o\in B
of a proper holomorphic family
X\to B
assigns to a tangent vector
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,o}
a Dolbeault cohomology class
\kappa^{p}_{o}(\tau)\in H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{o},T^{1,0}_{X_{o}})
on the central fiber of that germ. Any representative of the map acts on possibly twisted
(p,q)
-forms to produce
(p-1,q+1)
-forms. The square norm of this action appears in the formulas of both the curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
and the second fundamental form of
{\mathscr{H}}
in
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
. We begin by reviewing the definition of the Kodaira-Spencer map, and then discuss how it acts on sections of the BLS field
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
.

7.1.
Definition of the Kodaira-Spencer Map

Let
p:X\to B
be a holomorphic family over a domain
B\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
containing the origin. Let us recall the definition of the Kodaira-Spencer map

K^{p}_{o}:T^{1,0}_{B,o}\to H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{o},T^{1,0}_{X_{o}}).

There are some aspects of the construction that can be confusing, so let us be temporarily overly pedantic, and denote by
\bar{\partial}_{E}
the
\bar{\partial}
-operator of the holomorphic vector bundle
E
. Fix a
(1,0)
vector
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,o}
, extended to a holomorphic
(1,0)
-vector field on a neighborhood of
o
in
B
with the same notation
\tau
. Choose a lift
\xi_{\tau}
of
\tau
, i.e., a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X
such that
dp(\xi_{\tau})=\tau
. Since
p
is holomorphic,

dp\left(\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}\right)=\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{B}}dp(\xi_{\tau})=\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{B}}{\tau}=0,

and therefore
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}
is a
(0,1)
-form with values in the vertical vector bundle
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
. Now,
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}
is
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}
-exact, but in general it is not
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X/B}}
-exact. However,
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}
is
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X/B}}
-closed. This closedness is seen in local coordinates as follows. We take a chart with coordinates
s=(s^{1},...,s^{m})
on a neighborhood of
o
in
B
, and in terms of this coordinate chart we write
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
. By the inverse function theorem we can choose local coordinates
(z,t)
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
with
t^{\mu}=p^{\mu}=p^{*}s^{\mu}
,
\mu=1,...,m
. In terms of these coordinates we write
\tau=\tau^{i}(s)\frac{\partial}{\partial s^{i}}
and
\xi_{\tau}=F^{j}(z,t)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}+\tau^{i}(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{i}}
. Since the functions
\tau^{i}
are holomorphic,

\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}=(\bar{\partial}_{z,t}F^{j})\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}=\left(\frac{\partial F^{j}}{d\bar{z}^{k}}d\bar{z}^{k}+\frac{\partial F^{j}}{\partial\bar{t}^{\ell}}d\bar{t}^{\ell}\right)\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}.

From the formula

\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X/B}}H^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}=(\bar{\partial}_{z,t}H^{j})\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}

we see that

\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X/B}}\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}\xi_{\tau}=0.

To simplify notation, from here on we shall simply write
\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
for the
\bar{\partial}
-closed
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
-valued
(0,1)
-form obtained by applying
\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X}}
to the lift
\xi_{\tau}
of the holomorphic
(1,0)
-vector field
\tau
.

We claim that the Dolbeault class of
\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
in
H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{o},T^{1,0}_{X_{o}})
depends only on
\tau
, and not on the lift
\xi_{\tau}
. Indeed, suppose
\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}
is another lift. Then the global section
\eta
of
T^{1,0}_{X/B}\to X
defined by
\eta=\xi_{\tau}-\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}
satisfies

\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}=\bar{\partial}\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}+\bar{\partial}\eta,

and restriction to
X_{o}
shows that
[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}]=[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\tilde{\xi}_{\tau}]
in
H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{o},T^{1,0}_{X_{o}})
.

In fact, one can go further, and show that
[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}]
depends only on
\tau(o)
. Indeed, while
\xi_{f\tau}\not=p^{*}f\cdot\xi_{\tau}
for all
f\in{\mathcal{O}}(B)
, the difference
\xi_{f\tau}-p^{*}f\cdot\xi_{\tau}
is a section of
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
. Similarly
\xi_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}-\xi_{\tau_{1}}-\xi_{\tau_{2}}
is a section of
T^{1,0}_{X/B}
. These facts imply that the class
[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}]
is independent of the choice of holomorphic extension of
\tau
, and also that the dependence on
\tau
is linear.

Remark
.

If we choose our lift
\xi_{\tau}
so that it comes from a horizontal distribution then the linearity follows more easily, since in that case
\xi_{f\tau+\sigma}=p^{*}f\cdot\xi_{\tau}+\xi_{\sigma}
. In fact, in this case the linearity holds at the level of the forms
\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
, even before restrictions to the fibers of
p
.

\diamond

Remark
.

The choice of a
holomorphic
extension of the vector
\tau
is made for convenience, and is not required. Indeed, one can see from the local formula that for the lift
\xi_{\tau}
for each
t
the section
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}
is a
\bar{\partial}
-closed
T^{1,0}_{X_{t}}
-valued
(0,1)
-form. Again the class
[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}]
depends only on
\tau(o)
, but this time for slightly different reasons: the difference of two lifts
\zeta:=\xi_{\tau}-\tilde{\xi}_{\tilde{\tau}}
is vertical at
o
, and one checks that

\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}-\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}=\bar{\partial}_{T^{1,0}_{X_{o}}}\left(\zeta|_{X_{o}}\right)

by using a local computation as above.

\diamond

Definition 7.1
.

The linear map

(25)

K^{p}_{o}:T^{1,0}_{B,o}\ni\tau\mapsto[\iota_{X_{o}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi_{\tau}]\in H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{o},T^{1,0}_{X_{o}})

is called the Kodaira-Spencer map of the holomorphic family
p:X\to B
at
o\in B
.

In particular, if we choose any horizontal distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
then the maps

\kappa^{\theta}_{t}:T^{1,0}_{B,t}\ni\tau\mapsto\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\in\Gamma\left(X_{t},{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}\left(K_{X_{t}}\right)\right),

(see page
3.3
for the notation
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}
) represent the maps

K^{p}_{t}:T^{1,0}_{B,t}\to H^{0,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{t},T^{1,0}_{X_{t}})

in the sense of Dolbeault cohomology. In the proof of Theorem
2
these representatives will make another appearance, and it will be particularly important to choose the right horizontal lift. The next paragraph is concerned with the construction of this special horizontal lift.

7.2.
Action on the fibers of
{\mathscr{H}}

So far in this section we have been dealing with general holomorphic families
p:X\to B
. In the present paragraph we shall assume that the family
p:X\to B
is Kähler, i.e., is equipped with a
relative Kähler form
: a closed
(1,1)
-form
\omega
on
X
such that for each
t\in B
the restriction
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\omega
is a Kähler form on
X_{t}
.

The contraction of
E
-valued holomorphic
(n,0)
-forms on the fiber
X_{t}
of
p
with any representative of the Kodaira-Spencer class
K^{p}_{t}(\tau)
produces a
\bar{\partial}
-closed
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-form on
X_{t}
whose Dolbeault class is independent of the choice of representative of
K^{p}_{t}(\tau)
. Moreover, one has the following remarkable proposition.

Proposition 7.2
.

For a point
t\in B
and any holomorphic
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
f
on
X_{t}
the Dolbeault cohomology class
K^{p}_{t}(\tau)\lrcorner f\in H^{n-1,1}_{\bar{\partial}}(X_{t},E)
is primitive with respect to the Kähler form
\omega_{t}:=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\omega
.

Actually, one can do even better: one can choose a horizontal lift
\theta
such that

(26)

\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\omega=0\quad\text{for all $\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B}$}.

Using this lift, for any
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
u
on the fiber
X_{t}
one has

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\omega_{t}\wedge u)=(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\omega_{t})\wedge u+\omega_{t}\wedge(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u)=(\bar{\partial}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\omega)_{t})\wedge u+\omega_{t}\wedge(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{t}\wedge(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u),

which shows the following proposition.

Proposition 7.3
.

The form
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner u)
representing
K^{p}_{t}(\tau)\lrcorner f
is primitive.

If the relative Kähler form
\omega
is actually a Kähler form, i.e., in addition to being closed, it is also a positive
(1,1)
-form, then the horizontal lift defined in (
26
) is just the lift that is perpendicular to the fibers. It is easy to see that this lift is well-defined even if
\omega
is not positive. Indeed, the component of the lift
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}
that is transverse to the fibers is already determined simply because
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}
is a lift, so to determine the vertical component we need only
\omega_{t}
to be Kähler for each
t
, which is of course the case. More explicitly, using the implicit function coordinates
(z,t)
defined by
p(z,t)=t
, we can write

\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}=F^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{i}}+\tau^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{\mu}}.

Then
\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\omega=\left(F^{i}\omega_{i\bar{j}}+\tau^{\mu}\omega_{\mu\bar{j}}\right)d\bar{z}^{j}
, so taking

F^{i}:=-\tau^{\mu}\omega_{\mu\bar{j}}\omega^{i\bar{j}}

yields the desired conclusion.

8.
The proof of Theorem
2

From this point on in the article, we shall make use of an abuse of notation that will make formulas much more compact. As discussed in Section
3
, there are two equivalent ways to realize the sections of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
. One of them is as sections of
K_{X/B}\otimes E\to X
and the other is as equivalence classes of sections of
\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E\to B
. When applying the twisted Lie derivatives, we shall always use the second description. But we will abuse notation and write

L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}^{\theta}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})

to mean the section represented by the
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}^{\theta}}u
for any
u
representing the section
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
. Note that by Proposition
3.10
.a the resulting sections are independent of the choice of representatives.

8.1.
Formula for the Chern connections of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with Hermitian metric
h
. The BLS-Chern connection
\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}
for
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is given by the formula

\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}1,0}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left(L^{\nabla 1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right),\quad\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t},

where
\nabla
is the Chern connection for
(E,h)
(c.f. Definition
3.3
).

Proposition
8.1
will be proved momentarily.

There are many connections compatible, in the sense of Definition
2.8
, with the smooth structure of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
, including connections compatible with the almost holomorphic structures defined in Paragraph
4.2
, i.e., connections
\nabla
such that
\nabla^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
. We restrict ourselves to such
\bar{\partial}^{\theta}
-compatible connections, since it is among these that we shall find our Chern connections.

For every
p
-horizontal distribution
\theta\subset T_{X}
and every connection
D
for
E\to X
such that
D^{0,1}=\bar{\partial}
there is a connection
\nabla^{D,\theta}:\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))\to\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{1}_{B}\otimes{\mathscr{L}}))
defined as follows. Given a section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
, for each
\tau=\tau^{1,0}+\tau^{0,1}\in T_{B}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}=T^{1,0}_{B}\oplus T^{0,1}_{B}
the connection

\nabla^{D,\theta}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{f}}:={\mathfrak{i}}\left(L^{D1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau^{1,0}}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)+\bar{\partial}^{\theta}_{\tau^{0,1}}{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left(L^{D1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau^{1,0}}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})+L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau^{0,1}}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)

is compatible with the smooth structure of
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with Hermitian metric
h
. If
D
is a smooth connection for
E\to X
and
\theta
is a smooth horizontal lift then the induced connection
\nabla^{D,\theta}
is a connection for
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
in the sense of Definition
2.8
. Moreover, if
Dh=0
then
\nabla^{D,\theta}{\mathfrak{h}}=0
, where
{\mathfrak{h}}
is the
L^{2}
metric for
{\mathscr{L}}\to B
.

Proof.

The smoothness assertion is evident, and Lemma
3.6
shows that if
D
is the Chern connection for
E
then
\nabla^{D,\theta}
is the BLS-Chern connection.
∎

Proposition
8.2
immediately imply Proposition
8.1
.
∎

Remark 8.3

(Chern connection for
{\mathscr{H}}
when
{\mathscr{H}}
is locally trivial)
.

We have not defined the Chern connection for
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
when the latter is not locally trivial and we do not intend to do so. Of course, when
{\mathscr{H}}\to B

is
locally trivial, it has a well-defined Chern connection. Though we do not need the Chern connection for
{\mathscr{H}}
in our approach, we can now easily compute it from the Chern connection of the ambient BLS bundles
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}\to B
. Indeed, by Proposition
2.31
, Proposition
4.15
and Remark
6.11
one has the following proposition.

Proposition 8.4
.

If
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
is locally trivial then the Chern connection
\nabla^{{\mathscr{H}}}
of
{\mathscr{H}}
is smooth, and is given by
\nabla^{{\mathscr{H}}}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{f}}=\nabla^{{\mathscr{H}}1,0}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{f}}={\mathfrak{i}}\left({\mathcal{P}}\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)\right)

for any
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{H}}))
and
\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t}
, where
{\mathcal{P}}
is the fiberwise Bergman projection operator defined in Paragraph
6.5
and
\xi_{\tau}
is any lift of
\tau
to a
(1,0)
-vector field on
X_{t}
.

In
[
B-2009
]
the formula for
\nabla^{{\mathscr{H}}}
was derived directly using the definition of Chern connection. We note also that the formula for
\nabla^{{\mathscr{H}}}
makes sense for smooth sections of
{\mathscr{H}}
even when
{\mathscr{H}}
is not locally trivial.

\diamond

8.2.
The
\mathbf{(1,1)}
-component of curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}

By Proposition
2.48
and Remark
4.8
the restriction to
{\mathscr{H}}
fo the curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
has
(1,1)
-form coefficients. Since our interest is mainly in
\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}
, we shall only compute the
(1,1)
-component of
\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}
.

The
(1,1)
-component of curvature of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is by definition

(27)

\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}=\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma}\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\bar{\tau}}-\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\bar{\tau}}\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma}-\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{[\sigma,\bar{\tau}]},\quad\sigma,\tau\in T^{1,0}_{B,t},\ t\in B.

In the latter formula, one may take any extension of
\sigma
and
\tau
to
(1,0)
-vector fields, and taking holomorphic extensions eliminates the last term on the right hand side of (
27
). Therefore for smooth sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}
of
{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
we have
{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}\right)=\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}-L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\right){\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})
and

(28)

\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{i}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)=\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right),

where
u_{i}
are
E
-valued
(n,0)
-forms on
X
representing
{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}
.
Using (
28
) we can finally give the proof of Theorem
4.18
.

Proof of Theorem
4.18
.

Fix
t\in B
and smooth sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in\Sigma_{t}
, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}})_{t}
such that
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}(t),{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}(t)\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}u_{i}=0
, where
u_{i}\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
represents
{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}
.

Defining the vertical vector field
v_{\sigma}:=\xi^{\theta_{1}}_{\sigma}-\xi^{\theta_{2}}_{\sigma}
, we find that

\displaystyle\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{1}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)-\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{2}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)+\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right).

Now, one computes that

L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\tau}}u_{1}=L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\sigma}}(\bar{v}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1})=\bar{v}_{\tau}\lrcorner(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}u_{1})+\left(\overline{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}v_{\tau}}\right)\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u_{1}.

The first term on the right hand side vanishes because
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}\in\Sigma_{t}
, and the second term vanishes because
\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}\lrcorner\overline{\bar{\partial}v_{\tau}}
is a vertical
(0,1)
-vector field. Next, by Lemma
3.6
and the holomorphicity of
u_{i}
along
X_{t}
one has

\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)=-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}u_{1},L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\sigma}}u_{2}\right)=0.

Therefore

\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{1}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)-\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{2}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)=\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

Now,

\displaystyle\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)+\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{1}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)-\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\bar{\tau}\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},u_{2}\right)+(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2})+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}\left(u_{1},L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\sigma}}u_{2}\right)+(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2})+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2})+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right).

The last equality holds because
\bar{\tau}\left(u_{1},L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\sigma}}u_{2}\right)=\left(L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}}u_{1},L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\sigma}}u_{2}\right)+\left(u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{i}}_{\tau}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{v}_{\sigma}}u_{2}\right)=0
.

Now, since
v_{\sigma}
is vertical

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1}=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\nabla^{1,0}(v_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1})+v_{\sigma}\lrcorner(\nabla^{1,0}u_{1}))=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\nabla^{1,0}(v_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1})),

and hence
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1}\perp{\rm Kernel}(\Box^{1,0})={\rm Kernel}(\Box)={\rm kernel}(\bar{\partial})
, with the last two equalities holding because
\nabla^{1,0}(v_{\sigma}\lrcorner u_{1})
is an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form. Therefore

(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2})=(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2})=(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}),

and similarly
\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)=\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)
. Hence

\displaystyle\left(L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)+\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{v_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)+\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{v_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)-\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{1}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)-\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{1}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{1}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)-\left(P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta_{2}}_{\sigma}}u_{1},P_{t}^{\perp}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta^{2}}_{\tau}}u_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{1}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{1}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)-\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{2}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{2}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right).

Thus
\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{1}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)-\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta_{2}}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)=\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{1}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{1}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)-\left(\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{2}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},\mathbf{I\!I}^{\theta_{2}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}\right)
, as required.
∎

Theorem 8.5
.

For any
t\in B
and any smooth
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{L}}_{t}
one has the formula

(29)

\displaystyle(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2})_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}\left<\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}f_{1}\wedge\bar{f}_{2},h\right>-\int_{X_{t}}\left<(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma})\lrcorner f_{1}\wedge\overline{(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau})\lrcorner f_{2}},h\right>

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{0,1}}f_{1}\wedge\bar{f}_{2},h\right>+\int_{X_{t}}\left<f_{1}\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\tau},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}]^{0,1}}f_{2}},h\right>.

for the
(1,1)
-component of
\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}
. In particular, if
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
then

(30)

(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}f_{1},f_{2})_{t}=\ \int_{X_{t}}\left<\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}f_{1}\wedge\bar{f}_{2},h\right>-\int_{X_{t}}\left<(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma})\lrcorner f_{1}\wedge\overline{(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau})\lrcorner f_{2}},h\right>.

In the proof of Theorem
8.5
the following lemma is convenient.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a holomorphic submersion, let
v\in\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
be a smooth
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form, let
\sigma
be a holomorphic vector fields on a coordinate neighborhood
U
in
B
with coordinates
s=(s^{1},...,s^{m})
, and let
p=(p^{1},...,p^{m})
in these coordinates. Choose local holomorphic coordinates
(z,t)
on an open set in
p^{-1}(U)
such that
t^{i}=p^{i}
and, with
d\vec{z}:=dz^{1}\wedge...\wedge dz^{n}
, write

v=v_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}

for some local section
v_{o}
of
E
and local
E
-valued
(n-1,0)
-forms
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{m}
. Then

L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}v=(\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}v_{o})d\vec{z}+v_{o}\partial\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+\partial\sigma^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}+dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\alpha_{\mu}.

Proof.

We calculate that

\displaystyle L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}\left(v_{o}\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+\sigma^{\mu}\alpha_{\mu}-dt^{\mu}\wedge(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\alpha_{\mu})\right)+\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\left(\nabla^{1,0}v_{o}\wedge d\vec{z}-dt^{\mu}\wedge\nabla^{1,0}\alpha_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla^{1,0}v_{o})\wedge\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+v_{o}\partial\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+\partial\sigma^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}+\sigma^{\mu}\nabla^{1,0}\alpha_{\mu}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\nabla^{1,0}(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\alpha_{\mu})

\displaystyle+(\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}v_{o})d\vec{z}-\nabla^{1,0}v_{o}\wedge(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})-\sigma^{\mu}\nabla^{1,0}\alpha_{\mu}+dt^{\mu}\wedge(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\alpha_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}v_{o})d\vec{z}+v_{o}\partial\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+\partial\sigma^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}+dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\alpha_{\mu},

as claimed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.5
.

Fix a section
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
. Fix an
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form
u
representing
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
, and locally write
u=u_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}
in the coordinates of Lemma
8.6
. If
\sigma
and
\tau
are holomorphic vector fields on an open subset of
B
then an application of
L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}
to the formula for
L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}u
from Lemma
8.6
gives

\displaystyle L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\bar{\partial}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}u_{o}\right)d\vec{z}+(\bar{\partial}u_{o}({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}))\partial\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)-u_{o}{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner\left(\partial\left(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\partial\sigma^{\mu}\wedge\left({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}\alpha_{\mu}\right)+dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\alpha_{\mu},

where in the third term on the right hand side of the equality we have used the identity
\bar{\partial}\partial=-\partial\bar{\partial}
. Applying Lemma
8.6
to
v=L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u=(\bar{\partial}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u_{o})d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}\alpha_{\mu}
then yields

L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u=(\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u_{o})d\vec{z}+(\bar{\partial}u_{o}({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}))\partial\left(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+\partial\sigma^{\mu}\wedge L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}\alpha_{\mu}+dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}\alpha_{\mu}.

Therefore

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\bar{\partial}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}-\bar{\partial}_{{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}\nabla^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\right)u_{o}d\vec{z}+{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner(u_{o}\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}))+dt^{\mu}\wedge\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}-L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\right)\alpha_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u_{o}d\vec{z}+\nabla_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]}u_{o}d\vec{z}+u_{o}({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}))+dt^{\mu}\wedge\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}-L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}\right)\alpha_{\mu}.

Keeping in mind that
[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]
is a vertical vector field, we compute that

\displaystyle L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]}(u_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]\lrcorner\nabla(u_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu})+\nabla([\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]\lrcorner(u_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]\lrcorner(\nabla u_{o}\wedge d\vec{z}-dt^{\mu}\wedge\nabla\alpha_{\mu})+\nabla(u_{o}([\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]\lrcorner d\vec{z})-dt^{\mu}\wedge[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]\lrcorner\alpha_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]}u_{o}d\vec{z}+u_{o}\partial([\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]\lrcorner d\vec{z})+dt^{\mu}\wedge L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}\alpha_{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]}u_{o}d\vec{z}-u_{o}\partial({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}))+dt^{\mu}\wedge L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}\alpha_{\mu}.

Therefore, with
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})=u_{o}d\vec{z}+dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}\left(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}u_{o}d\vec{z}+L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})+u_{o}\left({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})+\partial({\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}))\right)

\displaystyle\quad+dt^{\mu}\wedge\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}-L^{0,1}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}-L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}\right)\alpha_{\mu}.

Now,

\displaystyle L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\left(u_{o}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}\left(u_{o}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)+\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\left(u_{o}\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla^{1,0}u_{o})\wedge\left(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)+u_{o}\partial\left(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)

\displaystyle+\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\left(\nabla^{1,0}u_{o}\wedge(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}+u_{o}\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner dz)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}u_{o})\left(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z}\right)+u_{o}\partial\left(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)+u_{o}\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner dz)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{o}\partial\left(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner d\vec{z})\right)+u_{o}\partial(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner dz),

L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}(dt^{\mu}\wedge\alpha_{\mu})=dt^{\mu}\wedge L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\alpha_{\mu}

and

L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})=\bar{\partial}(\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}))+\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})=(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma})\lrcorner{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}).

Since
L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})=\nabla^{1,0}(\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}))+\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})=0
because
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
and
\nabla^{1,0}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
are of bi-degree
(n,0)
and
(n+1,0)
, we have established the following formula.

(31)

\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})-[L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}},L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}]{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)+\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})\right)

for sections
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
.

Since
[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]
is vertical, if
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{f}}_{2}
are holomorphic on fibers then
L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{0,1}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})=0
, and similarly
L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\tau},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}]^{0,1}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})=0
. Thus (
30
) follows from (
29
), to which proof we now turn.

By Formula (
31
) we have

\displaystyle(\Theta^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}}_{\sigma\bar{\tau}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},{\mathfrak{f}}_{2})_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}\left<\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\right)\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>+\int_{X_{t}}\left<L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>.

Observing that
L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})
is an
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-form, by Proposition
3.8
we have

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{1,0}_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}L_{\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}}\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>-\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>-\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>,

the last equality being a consequence of the fact that
\left<L^{0,1}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>
is an
(n-1,n+1)
-form, and thus integrates to
0
on
X_{t}
. Finally, for reasons of bi-degree

\int_{X_{t}}\left<L_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>=\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{0,1}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>+\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{1,0}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{1,0}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>,

and by Lemma
3.6
and the fact that, since
\sigma
and
\tau
are holomorphic,
[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]
is vertical

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}\left<L^{1,0}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{1,0}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{X_{t}}\left<{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{\overline{[\xi^{\theta}_{\sigma},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau}]^{1,0}}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{X_{t}}\left<{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{1})\wedge\overline{L^{0,1}_{[\xi^{\theta}_{\tau},\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\sigma}]^{0,1}}{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{2})},h\right>.

This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem
2
now follows from Theorem
8.5
, Proposition
8.1
, Definition
2.47
.ii and Proposition
2.48
.
∎

9.
The Second Fundamental Form of
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}

We now turn our attention to the proof of Theorem
3
.

Let
\theta\subset T_{X}
be a smooth horizontal distribution. In the most general setting one cannot assume that
{\mathscr{H}}\subset{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}
is regular. Thus our second fundamental form, which by Definition
2.45
.ii is

\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}=P_{{\mathscr{H}}}^{\perp}\nabla^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}1,0},

does not depend smoothly on
t\in B
. The only term that is not smooth in
t
is the Bergman projection
P_{t}
, so while differentiation in the vertical directions is legitimate, we must be careful not to differentiate this projection with respect to
t
.

9.1.
A first formula for the second fundamental form

Our first result on the second fundamental form is useful in obtaining a more crude estimate for the curvature of
{\mathscr{H}}\to B
under the assumption that the metric
h
for
E\to X
is Nakano-positive along the fibers
X_{t}
.

Proposition 9.1
.

For every
t\in B
,
{\mathfrak{f}}\in\Gamma(B,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}({\mathscr{L}}))
satisfying
{\mathfrak{f}}(t)\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
, and
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}(B)_{t}
one has

\iota_{t}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\tau){\mathfrak{f}}=-G_{t}\bar{\partial}^{*}_{X_{t}}\left(\iota^{*}_{X_{t}}\left((\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\Theta(h))\alpha\right)+\left(\nabla^{1,0}\left(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\alpha\right)\right)+L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}\alpha\right),

where
\alpha
is any
E
-valued
(n,0)
-form representing
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}})
.

Proof.

Since
\iota_{t}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\tau){\mathfrak{f}}=P^{\perp}_{t}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\alpha
is orthogonal to
{\mathscr{H}}_{t}={\rm Harm}_{n,0}
, the Hodge Theorem implies that

\iota_{t}\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\tau){\mathfrak{f}}=G_{t}\bar{\partial}^{*}\bar{\partial}\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left(L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\alpha\right)\right),

and the result follows from Lemma
3.9
.
∎

9.2.
Direct proof of Corollary
1.2

We are now in a position to give the promised proof of Corollary
1.2
that does not pass through Theorem
1
.

The hypotheses of Corollary
1.2
provide a metric
h
for
E
such that
[\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}\Theta(h),\Lambda_{\omega}]
is positive definite for some relative Kähler form
\omega
. Fix
f_{1},...,f_{k}\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
and
\tau_{1},...,\tau_{k}\in{\mathcal{O}}(T^{1,0}_{B})_{t}
along which we will compute the curvature. Also fix a smooth horizontal distribution
\theta
.

By Corollary
5.3

{\mathscr{H}}
is locally trivial, and hence for every
f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
one can choose smooth sections
{\mathfrak{f}}_{1},...,{\mathfrak{f}}_{k}
of
{\mathscr{H}}
such that
{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}(t)=f_{i}
. Consequently if
u_{i}
are representatives of
{\mathfrak{a}}({\mathfrak{f}}_{i})
then
L^{1,0}_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}}\bar{\partial}u_{i}\equiv 0
.

Writing
\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau}{\mathfrak{f}}(t):=\mathbf{I\!I}^{{\mathscr{L}}^{\theta}/{\mathscr{H}}}(\tau){\mathfrak{f}}(t)
, we have by Lemma
3.9
and Proposition
6.9

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau_{i}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}(t),\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau_{j}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{j}(t)\right)=\left|\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau_{i}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}(t)\right|\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sup_{\gamma}\frac{1}{(\Box\gamma,\gamma)}\left|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left((\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i}+\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i})\right),\gamma\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sup_{\gamma}\frac{1}{(\Box\gamma,\gamma)}\left|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i},\gamma\right)\right)+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i},\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma\right)\right|^{2},

where the supremum is taken over all smooth
E
-valued
(n,1)
-forms
\gamma
. Now, for any
E
-valued
(n,1)
-form
U
and any
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-form
V

\displaystyle|(U,\gamma)+(V,\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|(U,\gamma)|^{2}+|(V,\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma)|^{2}+2|(U,\gamma)|\cdot|(V,\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}U,U)([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]\gamma,\gamma)+||V||^{2}||\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma||^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+2([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}U,U)^{1/2}([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]\gamma,\gamma)^{1/2}||V||\cdot||\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma||

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}U,U)+||V||^{2})(([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]\gamma,\gamma)+||\nabla^{1,0*}\gamma||^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}U,U)+||V||^{2})(([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]+\Box^{1,0})\gamma,\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}U,U)+||V||^{2})(\Box\gamma,\gamma),

where the first equality follows because
\Box^{1,0}=\nabla^{1,0}\nabla^{1,0*}
for
(n,q)
-forms, and the second equality is the Bochner-Kodaira Formula (
21
). Thus we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau_{i}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{i}(t),\mathbf{I\!I}_{\tau_{j}}{\mathfrak{f}}_{j}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma}\frac{1}{(\Box\gamma,\gamma)}\left|\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\left((\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i}+\nabla^{1,0}((\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i})\right),\gamma\right)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}[\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i},\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{j}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i},\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}})\lrcorner u_{j}\right)

Therefore by Theorem
2

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}(\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}_{\tau_{i}\bar{\tau}_{j}}f_{i},f_{j})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}}}f_{i},f_{j}\right)-\left([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i},\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{j}\right)

\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\int_{X_{t}}\sqrt{\text{\rm-1}}^{n^{2}}\left<(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i}\wedge\overline{(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}})\lrcorner u_{j}},h\right>+\left(\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}})\lrcorner u_{i},\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}})\lrcorner u_{j}\right)\right).

The estimate holds for all smooth horizontal distributions, but if we choose the horizontal lift of Proposition
7.3
then by Proposition
6.8
the last line vanishes, and we have the estimate

\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}(\Theta^{{\mathscr{H}}}_{\tau_{i}\bar{\tau}_{j}}f_{i},f_{j})\geq\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\left(\Theta(h)_{\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\bar{\xi}^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}}}f_{i},f_{j}\right)-\left([\Theta(h_{t}),\Lambda_{\omega}]^{-1}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{i}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{i},\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau_{j}}\lrcorner\Theta(h))u_{j}\right).

By Proposition
4.22
the right hand side is positive (resp. non-negative) as soon as
(E,h)
is
k
-positively (resp.
k
-non-negatively) curved. Thus Corollary
1.2
is proved.
∎

9.3.
Berndtsson’s Method of Representatives

In this section we present an adaptation of a construction of Berndtsson that is fundamental to the proof of Theorem
3
. In the case applicable to
[
B-2009
, Theorem 1.2]
Berndtsson integrated his method into the proof, and the crux of the argument is contained in
[
B-2009
, Lemmas 4.3 and 4.4]
. The technique was distilled further in
[
B-2011
, Lemma 2.1]
. In the case of
[
B-2011
, Theorem 1.2]
Berndtsson augments the method in
[
B-2011
, Section 4]
in a way that is hard to state in one or two sentences. The next result captures these two ideas of Berndtsson in a single result.

Theorem 9.2

(Berndtsson’s Method)
.

Let
p:X\to B
be a holomorphic family, let
E\to X
be a holomorphic vector bundle with Hermitian metric
h
, fix
t\in B
and let
\omega_{t}
be a Kähler form on
X_{t}
. Fix a horizontal lift
\theta
. For any smooth section
f\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(K_{X/B}\otimes E))
such that
\iota_{t}^{*}f\in{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
and any
\ell\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
such that
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}\ell=0
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\ell)\perp{\mathscr{H}}_{t}
for all germs of holomorphic vector fields
\tau\in{\mathcal{O}}(T^{1,0}_{B})_{t}\cong T^{1,0}_{B,t}
there there exists a representative
u\in H^{0}(X,{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\Lambda^{n,0}_{X}\otimes E))
of
f
(in the sense of Paragraph
3.1.2
) with the following properties.

a.

The
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-forms
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u)
are primitive.

b.

P_{t}^{\perp}\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}u)=\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\nabla^{1,0}\ell)
.

c.

The
E
-valued
(n-1,1)
-forms
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}(\xi^{\theta}_{\tau}\lrcorner\bar{\partial}u)
and
\iota_{X_{t}}^{*}((\bar{\partial}\xi^{\theta}_{\tau})\lrcorner f)
are in the same Dolbeault class.

Moreover, if
